PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

RECCiIiDS are like wills—as soon as they are made, 'there ib an effort to break them.

"FAINT heart never won fair lady," without considerable assistance on her part, ALL things come to those who know when to sthp waiting. IT would be a good thing if people could not play the violin until they know how.

No one is superstitious when it comes to receiv- ing the baker's dozen. WnEN a man's good deeds speak for themselves, he should not allow his voice to drown theirs. MIME are dozens of remedies for rheumatism, and it usually lasts long enough to give its victim a chance to try them all.

HARD as it is to climb up in the world, it racks us a good deal worse to climb down again.

IT happens frequently that the prophet' who is without honour in his own country can't afford to go abroad. "CAPITAL" CHAT.

On the question of the South African inquiry there are known to exist. relations, wt,ich may al- most be called strained between Lord Salisbury, and his Colonial Secretary. A denial at the present stage would no doubt, he plausible enough in view uf the fact that the Cabinet have not yet seriously discussed the form and nature of the African in- quiry. It is known, however, that consultations that have taken place unofficially and in private between Ministers, furnish little ground for the be- lief that anything like an agreement on the ques- tion is within the bounds of possibility. The d:ffer- once which has arisen between Lord Salisbury and Mr. Chamberlain is mainly the result ot a strong feeling which the Piime Minister has entertained throughout in regard to Mr. Chamberlain's diplo- macy. To this it is understood the Foreign Secre- tary attributes many of the difficulties that have arisen. Mr. Chamberlain, on the other hand, very strongly resents Lord Salisbury's interfereace.

The mail from America has brought a few fresh details about Harriet Beecher Stowe's last days. :41e was under the affectionate care of her two daughters when the stroke of paralysis which was the immediate cause of her death occurred. Her son is a Congregational minister in a town quite close to Hartford Up to the last two months Mrs. :Stowe retained her interest in others, and in fine weather was often out of doors. Friends would visit her, and sometimes would perform on the pianoforte. Till recently Mrs. Stowe herself would sit down before the piano and softly play some old tunes which she had learnt in childhood. her hand- writing was clear to the last, and for one so old her power to write graceful little notes was exceptional. It is probible that the famous address from British) women—bound up, as it is, in twenty-four octavo volumes—will eventually find its way to the Government at Washington for preservation among the national archives. It contains the signatures of hundreds of thousands of women.

There were at least four eminent ministers from Amenca or Canada ',reaching in London churches on Sunday. Dr. Amory Bradford, in the presence of a large number of his fellow-countrymen, com- menced his two month's occupancy of the pulpit of Kensington Chapel. At Marylebone Presbyterian Church, Dr. Lorimer made his welcome re-appear- snce. libs ornate style of preachirg is much ap- preciated by Dr. Pentecost's congregation. Dr. of Toronto, supplied the place of Dr. Mon- roe Gibson at the Presbyterian Church at St. John's Wood ; and the Rev. Principal Grant, also from Canada, officiated at the Scotch Church in Belgravia. Fresh arrivals of American divines are expected a little later on. Their preaching engage- ments do not by any means spoil their holiday, and in most cases enable them to secure it.

One of the difficulties which the Prince and Princess of Wales must have encountered in connec- tion with the marriage of Princess Maud has been to spread the patronage abroad among tradesmen as widely as possible. The honour, not to speak of the -,..rotit, arising from furnishing some part of the marriage trousseau makes the competition among the largest, firms as keen as it well can be, and their Royal Highnesses are doing their utmost to popu- larise themselves in this direction. Spits fl-his, with its old fame and reputation,has carried off i he privilege of weaving the wedding dress, a score of London firms have all received big orders, and the greater portion of the trousseau will be furni-hed by a well .known Portman-square establishment. Lon- don, therefore, should be satisfied.

It might be reasonably maintained that in mat- ters of observance of the law Parliament ought like Ctesar's wife, to be above suspicion. But this counsel of perfection is not lived up to. Instead of the makers of law being the strictest respectors of it, they assume to be above it, and arrogate the privilege of being not only its makers but it hreakers. This was brought out by the series of interrogations by which Sir Wilfrid Lawson has .ought to run to ground the question of the sale of intoxicating liquors on Parliamentary premises and ro ascertain by what authority this is done, for having previously unsuccessfully tried to bring to honk the Commissioner of Works and the Attorney- General, on Tuesday the facetious baronet turned upon Lord Stanley, Chairman of the Kitchen Committee. From him at last he got a definite admission. This is that the refresh- ment rooms of the Houses have not., and never had a licence, and that there is no intention of applying for one. Lord Stanley adds that he does not propose to take the opinion of the Law Officers of the Crown on the legality of the pres,-ty. free and easy arrangement. Under these circum- stances the attention of the Commissioners of In- land -Revenue may well be directed to what Sic Wilfrid Lawson describes as a business cen4ucte:l on the footing of a bogus drinking club. It seem, not impossible that they might get heavy penal- ties.

tan; shall complain thereof to tbe- oolobal of hie regiment. To this the prisoner begged to reply that he had considered the colonel of his regiment his persona 1 _ _

And here the evidence was closed, and the prose- cutor, prisoner and witnesses dismissed, and the court adjourned to meet again to deliberate with closed doors. It was a period of awful suspense with Traverse llocke. The prospect seemed dark for him. The rACT of the offenoe, and the Lew affixing the penalty of death to that offence was estab- lished, and as the Judge Advocate truly said, nothing remained but for the court to find their verdict, in accordance to both. Extenuating circumstances there were certainly; but extenuating circumstances were seldom ad- mitted in eourts•martial, the law and practice of which were severe, to the extent of cruelty. Another circumstance against him was the fact that it did not require an unanimous vote to render a legal verdict; but that if a majority of two- thirds should vote for conviction, the fate of the prisoner would be sealed. Traverse had but one friend in the court, and what could his single WAG@ do against so many 7—A.ppa.rently nothing; yet, as the prisoner on leaving the court-room raised his eyes to that friend, Herbert Greyson returned the look with a glance of more that encouragement— of triumph

"Sfr, the articles of war accord him that privi- lege. But is it ever taken advantage of? Is there a ease on record where a private soldier ventureu to make a dangerous enemy of his immediate superior by complaining of his captain to hui colonel. Nor in this case would it have been of the least use, Inasmuch as this soldier had well•founded reasons for believing the colonel of his regiment his personal enemy, and the captain as the instrument of this enmity." "And you, Major Greyson, do you coincide in the opinion of the prisoner? Do you think that there eould have been anything in common between the colonel of the regiment and the poor private in tue ranks, to explain such an equallsing sentiment as enmity?" inquired Captain O'Donnelly. "I answer distinctly, yes, sir I In the first place, this poor private is a young gentleman of birth and educatiou, the heir of one of the most important estates in Virginia, and the betrothed of one of the most lovely girls in the world. In both these capacities he has stood in the way of Colonel Le Noir, standing between him and the estate on the one hand, and between him and the young lady on the other. He has disappointed Le Noir both in love and ambition. And he has thereby made an enemy of the man who has besides the nearest interest in his destruction. Gentlemen, what I say now in the absence of Colonel Le Noir, I am pre- pared to repeat in his presence, and maintain at the proper time and place.' "But how came this young gentleman of birth and expectations to be found in the ranks?" Inquired Captain Rosencrantz. "How came we to have headstrong eons of wealthy parents, fast young men of fortune, and runaway students from the universities and colleges of the United States, in our ranks? In a burst of boyish impatience the young man enlisted. Des- tiny gave him as the colonel of his regiment his mortal enemy. Colonel Le Noir found in Captain Zuten a ready instrument for his malignity. And between them both they have done all that could possibly be effected to defeat the good fortune and insure the destruction of Traverse Rocke. In fact, I shall bring formal charges against Colonel Le Noir and Captain Znten, of conduct unworthy of officers and gentle- men r

"But it seems to me that this is not directly to the point at issue," said Captain Kingsley. "On the contrary, sir, it is the point, the whole point, and only point, as you shall presently see, oy attending to the facts that I shall recall to your memory. You and all present must, then, see that there is a deliberate purpose to effect the ruin of this young man. He is accused of having been found sleeping on his post, the penalty of which, in time of war, is death. Now listen to the history of the days that preceded his fault, and tell me if human nature could have withstood the trial?

"Sunday night was the last of repose to the pri- soner until Friday morningl when he was found asleep on his post. "Monday night he was sent with the reconnoitre- ing party to Casa-de-Mata. "Tuesday he was sent with the officer Vint carried our Ganeral's expostulation to Santa Anna. At night he was put on guard. "Wednesday he was sent with another party to protect a band of emigrants crossing the marshes. At night he was sent with still another party to reconnoitre Molino-del-Rey. "Thursday he was sent in attendance upon the

officer that carried dispatches to Gneril Quitmae, and did not return until after midn;uht, when, thoroughly worn out, driven indeed to Lea extreme degree of mortal endurance, he was again, on a sultry, oppressive night, in a still, solitary place, set on guard; where a few hours later be was found asleep upon his post —by whom 7—the colonel of his regiment and the captain of his com- pany, who seemed bent upon his ruin I—as I hold my- self bound to establish before another court mart al. "This result had been intended from the first! If five nights' loss of sleep would not have effected this. New& probably would; if fifteen would not, thirty would • or it thirty wouldn't, six'y would!— and all this Cuptain Zaten had the power to enforce until his doomed victim should full into the hands of the provost-marshal, and into the arms of death "And now, gentlemen, in view of all these cir- cumstances, I ask you—Was Traverse Rocke guilty of wilful neglect of duty in dropping asleep on his post? And I move for a reconsideration, and a new ballot!" "Such a thing is without precedent, air! These mitigating circumstances may be brought to bear on the Commander-in-Chief, and may be embodied In a recommendation to mercy! they should have no weight in the finding of the verdict," said the President, "which should be in accord.ance with the fact and the law." "Now are our brows bound with victorious wreaths, Our bruised arms hung up for monuments; Our item alarums changed to merry meetings, Our dreadful marches to delightful measures, Grim-visaged war hath smoothed his wrinkled front And now, instead of mounting barbed steeds, To fright the souls of fearful adversaries, He capers nimbly in a lady's chamber, To the lascivious pleasing of a lute." —SHAIEMPSARIL pursuant with the general orders issued from tteadguarters, the court martial, consisting of ' thirteen officers, convened at Tacubaya, for the trial of Traverse Rooks, private in the--Regi- east of Infantry, accused of sleeping on his poet It was a sultry morning, early in September, and by seven o'clock the drum was heard beating before the Arohiesoopal palace, where it was understood the trial involving We or death, would Gone off. The two sentinels on guard before the doors and a few officers off duty loitering about the verandahs, were the only persons visible near the well-ordered premises, until the members of the court martial, with the prosecutors and witnesses, began to assemble and pass in. Within a lofty apartment of the building, which was probably at one time the great dining-hall of the priests, were collected some twenty persona. comprising the court martial and its attendants. An extension-table covered with green cloth occupied the middle of tee long room. At the head of this table sat General W., the president of the court. On his right and left, at the sides of the table, were arranged the other members according to their rank. At a small table, near the right hand ef the President, stood vas Judge Advocate, or prosecutor on behalf of the United States. At the door stood a sentinel on guard, and near him two or three orderly sergeants, in attendance upon the officers. The Judge Advocate opened the court by calling over the names of the members, beginning with the President and ending with the youngest officer pre- sent, and recording them as they responded. This preliminary settled, orders were dispatched to bring the prisoner, prosecutor and witnesses into Court. And in a few minutes entered Colonel Le Noir, Captain Zaten, Lieutenant Adams and Sergeant Baker. They were accommodated with seats near the left hand of the President. Lastly, the prisoner was brought in, guarded, and placed standing at the toot of the table. Traverse looked pale, from the severe effects of excessive fatigue and anxiety; but he deported himself with firmness and dignity, bowed respect- fully to the court and then drew his stately form up to its fullest height, and stood awaiting the pro- ceedings. The Judge Advocate, at the order of the Presi- dent, commenced and read the warrant for holding the court. He then read over the names of the members, commencing as before, with the Presi- dent, and descending througn the gradations of rank to the youngest officer, and demanded of the prisoner whether he had any cause of challenge, or took any exception to any member present, and if so, to declare it, as was his privilege. Traverse lifted his noble head and keen eyes, and looked slowly around, in turn, upon each officer of the court-martial. They might all be said to be strangers to him, duce he knew them only by sight—all except his old acquaintance, Herbert Greysong who sat first at the lett hand of the President, and who returned his look of scrutiny with a gase of full encourage- ment. "I find no cause of cballencre, and take no excep- tion to any among the officeie composing this court," answered Traverse, again bowing, with such sweetness and dignity in tone and gesture that the officers, in surprise, looked—first at the prisoner, and then at each other. No one could doubt that the accused, in the humble garb of a private soldier, was nevertheless a man of education and refinement—a true man both in birth and breed- ing. As no challenge was made, the judge-advocate proceeded to administer to each of the members of the court the oath prescribed by the Articles of War, to the intent that they should try "the matter before them, between the prisoner and the United States, according to the evidence, without fear, favour, or affection." This oath was taken by each member holding up his right hand, and repeating the words after the officer.

CHAPTER LIII. THE VERDICT.

"We must not make a scare-crow of the law, Setting it up to frighten birds of prey; And let it keep one shape till nature makes it

Their perch and-not their-terror." —SHAKESPrang. The members of a court martial sit in the double capacity of jurors and jud.;es; as jurors they find the facts, and as judges they award the punish- ment. Yet, their session with closed doors was without the solemn formality that the uninitiated might have supposed to attend a grave deliberation upon a matter of guilt or innocence involving a question of life or death.

No sooner were the doors closed that shut out the "vulgar" crowd than the "high and mighty" officials immediately fell into easy attitudes, and engaged in conversation upon the weather, the climate, yesterday's dinner at General Cushion's quarters, the claret, the cigars and the Mexican signoritas. They were presently recalled from this easy chat by the President, a severe disciplinarian, who reminded them rather sharply of the basLless upon which they had convened. The officers immediately wheeled themselves around in the chairs, facing the table, and fell into order. _ _ _

The Judge Advocate seated himself at his detached stand, opened his book, called the atten- tion of the court, and commenced and read over the whole record of the evidence and the proceed- ings up to this time. The President then said: _

" For my own part, gentlemen, I think this quite a simple matter, requiring but little deliberation. Here is the fact of the offence proved, and here is the law upon that offence clearly defined. Nothing seems to remain for us to do but to bring in a ver- dict, in accordance with the law and the fact." Several of the older officers and sterner disciplin- arians agreed with the President, who now said: "I move that the vote be immediately taken on this question." To this also the elder officers acsented. And the Judge Advocate was preparing to take ballot, when one of the younger members arose and said: h Mr. P,esident and gentlemen, there are miti- gating circumstances attending this offence, which, in my opinion, should be duly weighed before making up our ballot." "Lieutenant Lovel, when your hair has grown white in the service of your country, as mine has, and wheta your skin is mottled with the scars of a score of well fought fields, you will find your soft theories corrected by hard experience, and you will know that in the case of a sentinel sleeping upon his post, there can be no mitigating circumstances; that nothing can palliate such flagrant and dan- gerous neglect, involving the safety of the whole army; a crime that martial law and custom has very necessarily made punishable by death," said the President sternly. The young lieutenant sat down abashed, tinder the impression that he had betrayed himselt into some act of gross impropriety. This was his first appearance in the character of juror and judge; he was lit,,rally "unaccustomed to public speaking," and did not hazard a reply. "Has any other gentleman any views to advance before we proceed to a general ballot 7" inquired the President.

Several of the officers whispered together, and then some or rsplied that there seemed to be no reason why the vote should not be immediately taken.

Herbert Greyson remained perfectly silent. Why he did not speak then, in reply to this adjuration,— why, indeed, be had not spoken be/ore, in support of Lieutenant views in favour of his friend, I do not know to this day; though I mean to ask him the first time I have the opplrt unity. Perhaps he was inclined to dramatic effects: but whatever might have been the motive, he continued silent offering no obstacle to the immediate taking of the vote.

_ The Judge Advocate then called the court to order for the taking of the ballot, and proceeded to question the members in turn, commencing with the youngest.

" How say you, Lieutenant Lovel, is the prisoner on trial guilty or not guilty of the offence laid to his charge 7" "GUILTY," responded the young oilloer, as his eyes filled with teats of pity for the other young life against which he had felt obliged to record his vote.

"If that is the opinion of one who seems friendly to him, what will be t'ae votes of the other stern judges!" said Herbeat Greyson to himself, in dismay. "What say you, Lieutenant Jones--is the pris- oner guilty or not guilty ?" said the Judge Advocate, proceedir with the bath. " Gui/ty "

LADIES' LETTER.

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

One very pretty hat I have seen this week WU made entirely of pink carnations, and this was trimmed with a bow of black velvet and a high brush osprey. These brush ospreys are a great comfort, for I never could bring myself to wear a real osprey after I learnt of the horribly cruel manner in which they are procured. It is perfectly simple to tell the difference between the real osprey and the brush osprey; the former are quite soft and very fine, the latter are stiff and have a thick centre stem.

Another hat most becoming is named the "Parisienne," with its blue straw brim, edged narrowly with the white straw which composes the high sugar-loaf crown, round which go three rou/e Tux of blue velvet; while at the side, a black and white osprey and two white wings uprise from a diminutive velvet bow.

Hairdressing of the simple, ordinary type has an immense influence on the appearance, and although variations in the prevailing styles are necessary to suit different features, yet unless these fashions are studied, the effect is not pleasing, and a fashionable hat somehow never sets properly with last season's style of hairdressing. Exaggerated fashions rarely obtain favour with us, and the plainly parted hair, waved, and brought over the ears, added 10 or 15 years to a woman's age, and was only copied by the few.

One of the most becoming hair arrangements that obtain now is the hand coil at the back of the head, with a curl fringe in front. A very simple but effective style for long, heavy hair, where a fringe has not been cut, and one or two small locks are cut short, curled, and allowed to fall over the foAhead. For the back, the Hovendon clip is admirable, and that with three top points prefer- able. The two centre puffs are arranged from the ends, and two fair-sized coils are required for this arrangement, and, if necessary, one could be added as previously explained. If you want a new gown to wear at garden parties, or at the seaside, or, in fact, to wear on almost any occasion until chill autumn comes, and warm clothing is a necessity? If so purchase im- mediately many yards of navy blue white pat- terned foulard. It is quite strange how the craze for foulards, crepe de chine, very high folded cors- let belts and jam-pot crowned hats has suddenly overtaken us almost at the end of the season.

A really beautiful gown is of blue shot brocade, with designs in pale maize-colour. This is made over a white satin vest and underskirt, which are s.ied with maize coloured chiffon, and the parasol is also of white satin, covered with frills of maize chiffon. The hat is of Straw the same colour, and it is trimmed with ("innings of chiffon and corn- flowers.

-- - Perhaps, however, a black- alpaca, canvas, or mohair gown may be already possessed in fair con-, dition, the bodice alone requiring brightening in honour of the forthcoming trip. If the bodice has been made with a vest of chiffon, soft silk or lace and revers of the material, it will be a pleasant change to entirely remove both vest and revers, and to substitute, a, yoke of pale pink silk figured with tiny black rings or dots, this yoke being carried right down to the waist as a pointed vest in front, but ant scriar, at the back. • Outline it all round with narrow black lace insertion thickly studded with sequins, finish the neck with a frill of pale pink chiffon turned out over a black satin band, and you will have a very .pretty although simple bodice. I Jatel saw a bodice which had undergone:a most succes,.!ful transformation carried out in this way. Originally it had had a short full basque, wide • revers of dark blue satin—the dress was of navy-blue canvas—and a full vest of 4cru lace, but the basque had been removed and the bodice cut short, blunt point back and front, carefully fitted, and finished with a nearly invisible piping of navy in ;in place- of the full vest the lace (carefully w i,thed and pressed) was sown over with tiny gilt bpanglp.s and laid- plain on a lining of cheap white satin; while on either side of it wide braces of blue ilnd' white striped glace ribbon were drawn up over the 'shoulder, loops of the same ribbon (tln.ce on either side) forming epaulettes, and falling over the tops of the sleeves.

Your loving JENrna

ME EXAMINER. SATURDAY JULY is, 1896.

Apropos, I am reminded of an mousing story, sent me by a Scotch friend, which is an exLelseut skit upon too modern tomato's for cheap things " ina.do in Gtrinany." Au old Scutcnman went one day into a Paisley LArd- ware store, and after inspecting the stock he asked toe salesman "Do you come free Germany ? " "No," plied the man," I was born in Paisley and my foiks be- tore me." " Weel,„said the old man, "ye ought to be proud o' yersel, for, as faur as I can see ye're the only hoe-utade article i'the whole shop."

Any subject in connection with weddings seems espe- cially welcome just now, and many of my readers who are not already aware of the fact, may be interested to know that bridal wreaths, were in vogue among the Pagans, and were introduced among Christiaa bride& during the fourth century. Nowadays in Prance an the United States, as well as Great Britain,the orange flower forms the bridal wreaths, the German bride Wf.+TS the myrtie, while pinks, carnations, and red roses ar, worn by Spanish brides. [he maidens of Italy and the I.,renck provinces oi Switzerlimo ir,e rue white rose, in Ncrway, Sweuen, and Servia °now CrOWLIS are made of silver, and in Athens of coSti) filagrt-C.

The scene in Buckingham Palace Gardens on Monday last, far outshone in epienuour anci magnificence any- thing ever seen there Letore. Princess Maud looked posicively radiant but the suadow of the coming parting bete ea herself and her sister, seemed already to have settled upon Princess Victoria. The old Mulberry Gar- gene much vaunted by Evelyn as a place where an " elegant " could get ruyaily fleeced, had, before their days of degeneracy,a certain charm and ostentation, but it is since Buckingham muse blossomed into a Palace that the gardens have grown in beauty, and quite one of the chief features of the day was the discovery that euch a glorious place was hidcien away in London. By the way, it is worthy of passing note that Buckingham ' Palace has the curious claim to remembrance that it was here the first cup of tea was brewed and drunk. The house was then the residence of the Duke of Bucking- ham and it was he who brouOt the first pound of tea into England and invited a group of friends to taste the decoction he himself brewea. - -

Speaking of the sea reminds me that people retiring from their annual outing, to houses thot have been shut up, are very apt to forget that the water in the pipes has had time in their aUmeuce not only to become stale but t..) get impregnated with ths lead of the pipes. It should be turned on full, and allowed to run freely before any of the water is used. Cisterns and boilers too should be cleared nut, for at this season of the year, insect life is most numerous, and a sight, after an absence of a few weeks, of the bottom of the family cistern, whence comes the supply of water for drinking and cookery, would be an eloquent argument on the aide of care and cleanliness.

The craze for embroidery and jewelling at the present moment is positively ruinous, and the only way to gain distinction, unless one's dress allowance is decidedly ample, is to dispense with this particular form of trimming altogether, and appear in severely simple gowns. Only the other day I saw a piece of jewelled embroiderv,destined to form the panel of a skirt and a little high pointed collar to match, which came to five guineas.—Even when our gowns are of some light and inexpensive material, the trimmings and the little details mount it up to something considerable in price. Men naturally do not realize this, and while they like to see their women folk always looking nice, and it is a di- light to them to hear their friends say "How charmingly your wife, or your daughter, as the case may be,dresses, they are apt to talk anything but pleasantly about ex- travagance, when the bills come in. Therefore those of us whose allowance is small will do well to dispense with these jewelled trimmings, (which unless of the best and consequently costly, should be avoided, sicmarpeifuill; avoided) and endeavour to gain distinction by ict and neatness. Many women there are, who devote much care and study to their gowns, but rarely spend much time or thought on their boots, shoes, gloves or handerchiefs. Now these are little items of the toilette which denote the gentlewoman. Our boots and shoes must always be suitable to the occasion, the buttons carefully sewn on, the laces in good condition. As to gloves, they should fit the hands and not be worn a BiZe too small, buttons should be securely in their place. and all bright colors or I stitchings shetild be eschewed. In the matter of i hander- 5, and great care as to chiefs, they must always be dainty, SC , nts exercised. Al those which are powerful should be strictly shunned. There is nothing fresher and more , delightful than the cld fashion, d lavender-water, and the fact that the Queen and the Princess of Wales invariably use it speaks for itself. Everyone has been complaining of the extreme heat, and useless as it is for those who seldom travel out of , sight of the city churches, there is little else to be done. At a company's meeting the other day in London, the Icl:airman recommended the American custom of fanning , and sent out for a supply of the inexpensive Japanese article, which was supplied to each attending share- holder. This led the more supposedly acute to anticipate an unsatisfactory statement of affairs from the chair- man, but his suggestion was simply out of sympathy with the general groaning in the spirit at the disagree- able temperature. Happily we never have anything quite so bad as tropical heat with the thermometer 900, er something over,in the hou,e, but it is quite as trying seeing that with trifling modifications most people are expected to get through as much in the day as in the other extreme conditions in winter. According to the French astrologers tie hot weather marks the approach of a coming comet, which may collide with the world and still further interfere with the even tenour of our ways, but this has been so often speculated upon that it is best to put off such apprehensions, as not many years ago induced some Americans to dispose of all their worldly goods in charitable ohj,.cts. Such spontaneous emotions as these have before now been described in the House of Commons as a kind of " fire insurance." Though the Doceased Wife's Sister Bill is not likely to become law this Session, the House. of Lords has now, for the first time passed it in all its stages. It would seem from the substantial majority of 38, by which the Upper House has sanctioned its third reading, that this change of opinion is not a casual circumstance, and that after the most persistent opposition for over 50 years the settled judgment of the House of Lords is now in favour of legalising marriage with a deceased wife's sister. The first attempt to effect this in Parliament was made in 1842, and the House of Commons has passed such a Bill at least five or six times since then, but it so happens that this year the Bill has not been dealt with in the Representative House, and unless the Government take it up it is said to have no chance of being further con- sidered this session. This, of course, just reverses the , old order of things. Up to now it has always failed be- cause the House of Lords would not pass it, and now this is overcome the difficulty is with the other House. Still under these altered Orcumetancee there is little doubt that before long the Bill will become the law. In its present form it has been relieved of the amendments with their more objectionable features, permitting the clergy to stigmatise such marriages, and it would, if passed, be retrospective in effect. The Prince of Wales and members of the Royal Family, with seats in the House of Lords, have alieady assisted the supporters of the Bill, and there is not the least doubt that popular opinion, especially amoog the poorer classes,is in favour el this long delayed reform.

CYCLISTS AND THE CHESHIRE POLICE.

The recent raids by the Cheshire police on cyclists has assumed a curious phase on the road leading past tbe Altrincham Police Station, in the direction of Manches- ter. Several persons have been fined for not giving audi- ble notice of their approach to horses or those in charge of vehicles, and on Sunday evening the various cyclists sought to comply with the law in rather a facetious fashion by having old-fashioned house bells with springs attached to their machines, which kept up a continual jingle as they went along. In two instances about 100 cvcli-its came along, and what with continuous blowing of whistle-I and the bells a noise approaching pinde- ironiurn was produced. The riders appeared to highly enjoy this novel method of complying with the law.

THE BRIDESMAID'S PERIL.

In the village of Newhey, between Rockdale and Old- ham, there lives at 3, Travis-st., Mrs. Maria Crossley. Having resided there ten years,che is known and highly respected by the inhabitants.

Twelve months ago her daughter was taken ii!, and despite the care bestowed upon her by her mother and sister, became worse, until a physician in consultation with the family doctor declared that there was no hope of her recovery. In this extremity the anxious parent, having witnessed the beneficial effects of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, put them to the test, with the result that her daughter has now recovered,whilst at one time she had to be wheeled about like a child. To a " Rochdale Times " reporter Mrs. Crossley said :

"About twelve months ago my daughter Polly was I taken ill, and was obliged to come home. I sent for a I doctor, who said she had erysipelas in her body. He continued to attend her,but she did not seem to improve, and in October last year he said it was no use his calling any more. She had five abscesses on one leg and many on her face, and for months was unable to walk or raise harself. She lest her appetite and could not sleep for pain. Her sister slept near her for five months. About Christmas I got some of Dr. Williams' ?ink Pills. When she had taken two or three bores she began to feel the benefit, and with a little help could walk across the floor atter being unable to walk for nine months. She con- tinued to take the Pills, and gradually gained strength. Her leg has healed up beautifully. A few weeks ago she was bridesmaid for the sister who nursed her." " Her recovery is due solely to the Pills ?" " Cer`ainly ! She has had no other medicine. I re- commended a woman whom I saw to get some for her daughter, who had been ill for years. She followed my advice, and says they have done her daughter a lot of good. I told the doctor so,and he said he was not going to run them down, as there was a great deal of strength in them.

The reporter spoke to several people, who confirmed in every particular the story told him by Mrs. Crossley, and he found Dr. Williams' Pink Pills praised amongst all classes, as a strengthening and tonic medicine, for men, women or children. They are not like other medi- cine, nor can they be imitated, as i sometimes dishon- estly pretended ; take care that the pickage bears the full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People,aed in case of doubt send direct to Dr. Williams' Mc, l'icine Company, 46, Holborn-viaduct, London, E.C., as the Pills can be had post-free at 2s. 9d., or six box :s for 13a. 91. They have cured numerous cases of erysife,las, influenza, uremia, palpitations, and the disorders which arise from impoverished blood, such as muscular weak- ness, loss of appetite, shortness of breath, pain in the back, nervous headache, early decay, all forma of female weakness, hysteria, paralysis, locomotor ataxy, rheu- matism, and sciatica.

CHESHIRE LINES ATHLETIC FESTIVAL.

On Saturday the Cheshire Lines annual athletic festi- val was held at Southport, and the occasion provided enjoyment for fully 5,000 representatives of all branches of the Cheshire Lines service,accompanied by their rela- tives and friends. The afternoon was gloriously finNand the sports,held in the Southport Athletic Society's splen- did new grounds, proved and unualified success. The prizes, which formed an elegant and costly display as they stood grouped together in readiness for presenta- tion, were handed to the successful competitors by Mr. David Meldrum, manager to the Che- shire Lines Committee. Accompanying Mr. Meldrum were a number of invited guests, including— Mr. T. J. Hulse of Warrington. Afterwards tea was provided in a spacious marquee, and the return trains departed sufficiently late to permit of enjoyable rambles through the Kew Gardens, and a brief but pleasant visit to Southport. Praise is due, for their admirable arrangements, to the committee, of which body Mr. William Beaver was chairman; Mr. E. P. Connolly, honorary secretary; Jr. S. Abrams, honor- ary treasurer. Efficient services were rendered as judge by Mr. J. G. Jones, and as starter by Mr. J. Sharp. The Cheshire Lines Band, directed by Bandmaster Thomas Meridith, rendered most eajoyable sel,ctions while the sports were in progress. NEW BTJOKS, MA.GAIINES, ETC.

CASSELL AND CO.—" Cassell's Family Magazine" should this mouth secure a fresh circle of readers for the current number is a more than usually excellent one. The first nine pages are occupied by an article from the pen of D. 11. Parry on" Our Army; Past and Present," which makes very inter- esting reading. Mr. Parry deals with the changes of equipment in the British soldier, from his crea- tion, little more than 200 years ago, to the present day ; but this is not all. Numerous anecdotes are interwoven with the descriptive portions and facts generally unknown are related. A num- ber of illustrations add to the attraction of the article.—The additional chapters of Max Pember- ton's story," A Puritan's Wife" which commenced in the previous number, serve to prove that the writer's fame as a producer of romantic and excit- ing narrative, is likely to be streng.thened by this last venture. There is also in this number of the magazine a capable descriptive article on " Lord George Sanger's Circus " by "one who has travelled with it," and the other items are of the usual char- acter.— X sixpenny guide to London has emerged from the publishing-house in Ludgate Hill, this month. It contains 10 plans,numerous illustrations, and more particulars than a visitor to the metro- , poles would ever master. This is not a draw-back I however, as the more one knows and the easier it becomes to surmount obstacles which are not slow at presenting themselvee to the" country cousin " gone up to town. The Guide should be a useful ad- junct to the sight-seer's paraphernalia.—The July number of "Little Folks" commences a new volume of this popular children's paper. It con- tains the opening chapters of two new serials "The Tale of a Tambour" by D. H. Parry, and "The Surprising Adventures of 'Tunny and Tue." by Maggie Browne. The stories are of widely different character and should meet the desires of all classes of readers. Sixteen of the pages of thid number are printed in colours and lend additional brightness to the contents.—In this month's " Magazine of Art " is given a supplement contain- ing taste lul reproductions of notable pictures hibited at the Paris salons. MACMILLAN AND Co,—" Melincourt, or Sir Oran Haut-Ton," by Thomas Love Peacock, is the latest of the Illustrated Standard Novels. Mr. G. Saints- bury writes the introduction, in which he explains why in the strictly comparative sense" Melincourt" is considered the least interesting of Peacock's novels. Illustrations to the work are from the pen of Mr. F. H. Townsend, and with them no fault csn be found.—" The Lover's Tale," "To Alfred Tenny- son," and " The First Quarrel," together form an- other part of the Tennyson People's Edition," which has now reached almost a score of pretty little volumes.—A most complete and learned book on "The Scenery of Switzerland, and the causes to which it is due," has its author in Sir John Lub- bock, Bars., M.P. In his preface the author says "In the summer of 1861 I had the pleasure of spending a short holiday with Huxley and Tyndall. . . . From that day to this many of my holidays have been spent in the Alps. . . . My attention was from the first directed to the interesting prob- blems presented by the physical geography of the country. I longed to know what forces had raised the mountains, had hollowed out the lakes, and directed the rivers. Our know- ledge is very incomplete. Nevertheless an immense kind of information has been gathered to- gether and even where authorities differ a short statement of their views could hardly fail to be in- teresting and instructive." After failing to per- suade others to write such a volume,Sir John says ho undertook the task under their encouragement and assistance. Professor Heim and Sir John Evans have both looked _through the proofs and made valuable suggestions. The work deals sp ci- ally with the valleys of tee Arye, Rhone awl Rhine, the Reuss, Aar, Limmat and Ticino as typed of longitudinal and cross valleys; and because they are the districts most frequently visited. Sir John apologises for the imperfections of the book, which is nevertheless a most valuable contribution to the general knowledge on the subject, and one especi- ally welcome as there is no other in existence de- voted to the explanations of the physical geo- graphy of Switz Aand. The publishers have issued the book in a serviceable and neat cloth cover while the text is printed in large cfpar type. At the end is a splendid map of the Republic. SMITH, ELDER AND Co —With the current num- ber of the " Cornhill Magazine " anew series be- gins under new editorship. The magazine is in- creased in size to 144 pages. and reverts to its old price of Is. After the 'Conclusion of Mr. W. E. Norris' serial story, "Clarissa Furiosa," the mega- sine will contain a new serial by Mr. Henry Beton Merriman, the author of "The Sowers," "With Edged Tools," &c. to be succeeded in turn by novels from Mr. Stanley Weyman,the author of "A Gentle- man of France," and Mr. S. R Crockett, the author of " Cleg Kelly " and "The Raiders." The new ven- ture is commenced by an article on "The first Number of 'The Cornhill,'" by Mrs. Richmond Pitchie, who relates many interesting particulars in connection with the " launch " of the good ship "Cornhill." Some interesting correspondence be- tween Thackeray and other writers of eminence is published in the article, which also in a measure, follows the course of the " Cornbill" up to the time of Mrs. Ritchie's father ceasing to be its editor. The familiar cover of the journal is adhered to,and may for many more years enclose reading matter famous for its literary excellence. GEORGE NEWNES AND Co.—The most notable item in the contents of the" Strand Magazine " for July, is an illustrated article by Alfred T. Story on "Mr. Andres's Balloon Voyage to the North Pole." The origin of Mr. Andres's idea is told, and he balloon fully described. A description is given of the balloon observatory and in fact of everything taken by the adventurous aeronaut on his voyage. Portraits are re-produced of Mr. Andros himself and his co-voyageurs, Dr. Nils Ekholm and Mr. Nils Strindberg, and also of Captain H. Q. Zschau, com- mander of the " Virgo" which took the balloon to Spitzbergen. There is also a map given showing the starting point in Spit zbergen and the various courses possible, and a copy of the circular distri- buted over Si beria,giving the inhabitants directions how to assist the voyagers in their descent. Altogether the article will be found to be one of the most interesting of the many published in "The Strand."—ln "The Strand Musical Magazine 'is published the result of the May prize competition and particulars are also given of another three guinea competition which should prove an attrac- tion.

A BIG HOTEL SCHEME FOR

11 ANCHESTER.

--- It is announced that the Midland Railway Company contemplate establishing in Manchester one of the finest hotels in the kingdom. The company have within the last few days secured the Gentlemen's Concert Hall, the Dile of warehouses between the hall and Mount-street, ind the buildings on the lefthand side of the Mlunt- street approach to the Central Railway Station. In fact, the cmloany will shortly come into possession of the whole blnck which has for its bounds reter-itreet, Lower Mosley-street, Windmill-street, and Mount-street. The scheme of the new hotel will include several features of great, if not unique, interest, among them being the construction ot o, hall suitable for concerts, entertain- ments. and banquets.

WARRINGTON TRLINING

COLLEGE.

ANNUAL PRIZE DISTRIBUTION.

THE DEAN OF MANCHESTER ON THE EDUCATION

QUESTION. On Saturday the prizes won by students at the War- rington Training College were distributed by the Dears of Manchester. There was a very large attendance. All tbe scholars were present, being accommodated on a spacious platform at one end of the room. Here the chair was taken by the Dean of Manchester, who was supported on the platform by the Revs. Morley Steven- son (principal of the Training College), Canon Penrhyn, Canon Willis, Canon Rowntree. Mr. S. G. Haworth, and Mr. J. It. Buckton. The PRINCIPAL said there were all kinds of curious notions amongst people as to the objects of training col- leges. He read not long ago, with regard to education, a speech made by a Liverpool Nonconformist minister, he thought of the name of Aked ; and he said that he thought all the training colleges, and more especially the Church of England training colleges, were a sort of con- ventual institution, where they had no amusernents,and in which they were tyrannized and dominated over by priestly intolerance. (Laughter.) He (the Principal) thought they had the answer to those remarks ie what he saw in front and behind him. (Laughter.) It alight appear well to ask themselves what was their obj3ct in such places as these, and how they were training the students. He wanted to say a word about what they did physically. He thought they were very much inclined to believe that in training teachers they neglected the physical state of their educational training T, la year:theelhad been trying toremedy this rather m 7.. than they had done before. They bad started a tennis club, and, they were rather vigorous about tennis. (Hear, hear.) They had started a bicycle club, and one of them had had an accident that rather marred her beauty. (Hear, hear.) It was a startling innovation upon their conservative notions. He believed it was a most healthy and most invigorating exercise, and he hoped that a girl who learned to cycle there well would after a hot day in school get on her bicycle and go for a twenty mile run and come back all the better for it. With regard to the training of teachers,they would have seen within the last few years there had been a decided I change upon the theory of training. When first he be- , came the Principal of that College they were contented with a different kind of education than they were hay- ing at the present moment. The whole tendency had I been upward. The whole tendency had been to uplift the studies of the teacher into a higher region than they had been heretofote. They saw it in the syllabus. They saw it in the encouragement that was given and the attention of the teacher for the students to pass the examinations. This he knew was what was determined upon by the wise people and by the people who insisted upon these things being done thoroughly. They of course must follow in the steps whieh they had marked out for them, and the whole of 'their education there was receiving an impetus to take higher and deeper views of study than they had done hitherto. The change was most weleeme. They were going to see before two years were over thet the education of their pupil teachers would be very different than it was at the present moment. They were going to enter the best of their students for Uni- versity examination. The nine best girls who would enter the College next Septemaer would begin to do university work. They were mindful of the best, and they were not unmindful of the others. They had been brought into touch with the Victoria University, and they had made arrangements by which they would have all the four courses of lectures from professors in that university. Those lectures they had very much en- joyed, and they had been productive of the very best possible results to their students. Physical culture and intellectual culture could in their way make a perfect teacher, still they wanted something higher and bet'er than those. They must have the spiritual side as well. (Hear,hear.) This was a more difficult thing te speak of, and he was about to say that it was recognised by those who were able to judge that the tone of • that College was high. (Hear, near.) It had been witnessed to, not only by the inspectors, whose judgment probably might be more in leaning in that direction than those of other people ; but it hid been witnessed by the Government Inspector, Mr. Gar- nett-,and they were satisfied as to the high spiritual tone which had characterised the College. He could not tell them what the College would be without the chapel. He could only tell them thie,that from the simple but digni- fied and very reverent services in their chapel flowed a stream of influence that brightened itself throughout the life of all of them there. Almost every stusent living within that College in a few de ys had obtained a post, and the two or three who had not were waiting for posts in particular localities. (Applause.) He was very sure of :this, that within the nf xt few weeks, if he had an- other batch of students as nice and good—(laughter)— he could place then; all,and he only wished he could un- der these:circumstances take as many more and supply the demand which existed.He thought he had stood long enough between them and the Dean of Manchester,who, as they all knew, took a great interest in educational matters. More than all, they must know what a friend he had been and was to the College. He was the secretary of their committee, but he was not the secre- 'arc- in name only—he took a deep interest in their work and was alwaysreadY to help them with his counsel and in every possible way. (Applause.) The DEAN of MANCHESTER said that the previous speaker ea i truly said that no one could take a deeper ietere,t in that College than he did himself. Among the charees and business laid upon his time and attention he candidly owned that there was none he preferred to the interests of the Training College at Warrington. He had very good reason for making that assertion and he slieuld tie un worthy of the post of their hon. sec. if he did not recognise and state there his high appreciation of the constant services during the oast—now about 13 years—ot the Principal. (Hear, hear and applause.) It was an old saying that "As lithe master so is the school As is the principal so is the college." Owing to the great improvements in everything that ceplipped the college in its various appointments, eithr as regarded buildings ur other necessaries, they were in debt to the sum of £1,300 to someone or other—either the hanker or the tintiespeople. He asked the repor- ters to teke a note of that and allow the good people in the clieepee to be fully alive to the fact that they wanted £l3OO at least to rid themselves of their pecuni- ary diaadvantages. He rejoiced to be able to confirm what the Principal had stated. He would also add that Mr. Scott Coward, on his last inspection, had informed the meinbers of the committee that they were dependent upen the Principal himself for the tone which character- ised this college as a whole. For that they could not be too thanl:ful or too cordially express their obligations to the :trincipal,—(Applause )—who was fully aware of what devolved upon him as the head of that great insti- tution. He (the Dean) would himself never rest until he saw the whele of those buildings devoted to the work of a Church Training college. He trusted that time would surely come. ,ar, hear.) He added that he wanted to speak to them upon that very critical position of elementary education. He was glad himself—though he was at one time disposed to be sorry—that the Bill of the Government had been withdrawn. (Bear, hear and applause.) He thought it was toe heroic by three-fourths. (Flear, hear and laughter.) It compassed too much. (Applause.) It was very difficult to comprehend, and he had lately the duty devolved upon him in convocation to attempt something like an anslesia ef it •, and it was a very drastic jet). The Bill was so badly drafted and was somewhat clumsily contrived, that it was difficult to see one's way through it. (Hear. hear.) It was an entirely new departure and it progoosticeted what Mr. Gla:isroee considered to be a revolution of the element- ary edurational system. He (the Dean) would not have cared much about that provided there was a proper autlaority in different localities, but it got into such very narrow dimensions that at last one fine night—he sup- posed it was from the heat—Mr. Balfour gave away the Government all in a hurry to a constitution of some 290,000 people Then he thought it was all up with the Gevernment Bill. What he had always felt about the gtiesfien of elementary education was that inasmuch as in 1870, which was called the year of compromises, he could never understand what the meaning of this coin- prewse should be. He knew somethirg of the history of that compromise and it was a compromise on such limes that there were to be two systems side by side, deacribed generally as the Voluntary—he preferred to call it the Denom,itational sestem—and the Shoal Board system, anti those two systems were then aid there crystallised and had existed during 25 years. Those two systems existed at the present mement ; they had not grown one upon the other so much except in certain districts. This showed that the denominational sestem had held its owe speak- ing for the country generally. Tnere we re ceetain por- tions of the country where the denominational system lied hid to take a back seat-. notacly, he was sorry to say, in London i'self.where the proportion of voluntary scholars to those of the schaels of the School Board was very sadly different from what they could wish. It was net so in Lancashire and in the great towns of Lanca- shire. In the great cant'' it populationin Lencaehire, th mela there had been et shrinkage,yet en the whole tbe dennininationel sysa sill held the field to a large xtent, and lt said that o' the nation had endorsed the princiole cf a dual systee ie education, each part of the system ought to have fen. play. (Hear, hear.) Fair play all ronnd, fair play ie every resource, and it was net for him to try what a many &ever people tried—to

He !Moped the time would cerne—aed he !believed it would come. this eutumn—that there should be through the clerical and lay heads of the church coming:to agreement, when the Elementai-y system connected with the Deno- mination should continue to have 'ts place arnongt them, and by which, as he theught, a true religious liberty of the people should be ensured all round. The author of "The Heavenly Twins," (whose real name is Mrs. Haldane M'Fall) was reared, so to speak, in an artistic as well as in a good social atmosphere. She possesses a law musical voice, and a very quiet gentle 'Timmer. Madame Sarah Grand rises early in the morning and devotes from eight to three o'clock to her intellectual pursuits. She finds that playing Beethoven or Ch pin is the most fruitful stimulator of thought, but she cannot work well in town, she loves to retire Onto the country and to be noar the sea.

ALL MEN ARE LIARS.

.8 ).writes a Pop,'lar Author,

Not so bad as that I hope, although I must admit some men are wicked liars ; in fact, it's only by accident they ever speak the truth. It is not a lie when Gershon, the Universal Furnisning Contractor, 44, Oldham-street ; 17, 19, and 21, Hilton-street, Manchester, states that he never attempts to put before the public common new London made rubbith, more like orange boxes, for high-class second-band; it is not a lia when Gershon states that he never attempts to trespass upon his neighbour's business. Why should he? It only causes a bad feeling. Gershon's motto is live and let live. He sticks to the one thing he understands, namely, furnishing. If you can't furnish with a man that understands his business, you cannot do so with men that don't. Gershon has for years cautioned the public against buying common new London furniture for second hand, and he still does so. However distasteful it may be to others, Gershon still takes his stand upon the rock of " Honesty and Justice," which are the guiding principles of his business, and from vihich precepts time nor tide will cause him to recede. The bathing of boys in placPs at art isolated distance from any thoroughfare is the most pregnant cause of death by drowning. The youngsters who are liable to cramp, who get out of their depth, or who lose confidence even when able to swim, are too often drowned through no assiA- anco being at hand to save them. In a public bath the danger is greatly reduced as there are always swimmers with their eyes on the juveniles.

The Cycling Club at Runcorn holds next week a carnival night. The proceedings embrace a proces- sion, which, judging from the entries, should be a great:spectacular success,and a concert to conclude, for which several capable artistes are announced. The sole object for which the club has promoted the carnival is to inaugurate a fund for the building of a Cottage Hospital in the town. This necessity, above all other necessities, has long been felt though no one seems up to now to have had the re- quired energy to bring the matter to a tangible issue. The cyclists of the town should therefore receive credit and suppo,rt in their spirited action in coming forward and setting the financial ball I rolling. They hope to realize a round sum and to give everyone an opportunity of subscribing the " littles " which make a " muckle." Collecting boxes have been distributed and set up in every convenient and prominent corner of the town.

The Ladies' Club at Halton is to be congratulated on the successful celebration ef the 80th annivers- ary of the friendly Society. Nowadays we hear a great deal of the new woman who claims the right to look after herself but the good ladies at Halton seem to have been emancipated for a matter of 80 years at the end of which period the'club's prosper- ity is sounder than ever. According to Mr. Reynolds, whose valuable services as trustee to the club are now at an end, the Society 25 years ago had £750 invested, while at the present time the funds invested amount to close on .£1,500. Tuts is satisfactory progress and if the uresent position of the Society is any criterion it should have a long and useful career.

THE WARRINGTON SWINDLING CASE.

SENTENCE ON GRAN C.

John Garlick alias John Grant, 37 Years of age, was on Tuesday, at the Liverpool Quarter Sessions, indicted for having at Warrington on the 17th April last, unlaw- fully and knowingly, by certain fake pretences,obtained from William Stockton, the sum of £6O in money, and on the 4th May obtained from Margaret Ann Ashmore, the sum of £5O with intent to defraud. Me. J. W. Ross Brown, instructed by Mr. Arthur Browne, of Warring- ton, prosecuted, and the prisoner was represented by Mr. Bleckwood Might, instructed by Mr. F. R. Roberts, of Warrington. In stating the case, Mr. ROS 9 Brown said that in con- sevence of being out of employnieet and hearing that prisoner fad work to offer, Stockton went to him on the 17th of April at Warrington. Complainant had an inter- view with him at his place of business in Lythgoes-lane, and alleged that the prisoner informed him that he was a man of substance but wanted more capital to put in his business. He showed Stockton the place where a good businese was apparently being done, twenty or thirty hands being employed. Prisoner it was al:e, • ' added that he had no debts, and had money in the Lan- cashire and Yorkshire bank at Warrington ; also that he had a contract with Mr. William Dear!, grocer, of Warrington, to the extent of £6O a month. On the strength of these representations, Stockton agreed to en- ter into his employ and granted him £6O, he to receive 30s. a week wages and 7i per cent. interest on the ad- vance. He was only in the prisoner's employ some ten days when the works were closed. It was afterwards discovered that the prisoner had no banking account on the 17th of April and had no contract of £6O a month with Mr. Isaac. In the case of Mrs. Margaret Ann Ash- more, complainant replied to an advertisement in a Liverpool newspaper, offering employment to anyone that could put money in the business,and ultimately en- tered the prisoner's service at 16e. a week as his clerk. She advanced him £5O at 5 per cent. interest. She had ; not been in his service more than a week when becom- ing suspicious she issued a warrant for prisoner's arrest, and he was arrested at Wigan. William Stockton gave corroborative evidence and wae cross-examined at length by Mr. Blackwood Wright. Other evidence was also given by Mr. Gilbert, of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Bank, Warrirwton ; Mr. Robert Lowe, Mr. Charles Lyon, of the " Liverpool Echo." and Detective Sergeant Monaghan.—Mrs. Ash- more was then called, and gave evidence as to the facts stated in her case by counsel. In cross-examination by Mr. Blackwood Wright, she admitted that what had led her to lend prisoner the money was the hope of employment. On this point the magis•rites gave their opinion that with regard to the charge brought by Mrs. Ashmore there was no case against the prisoner. At the close of the evidence the counsel for the prose- cution and defence addressed the jury. Mr. Black woted Wright for the prisoner, in a long and n hie speech ar- gued that all that could be alleged against the prisoner was that he had traded reeklesslyeind that as the magis- trates had declared that Mrs. Ashmnre's cheree was not substantiated, and that Stockton's charge having been brought after Mrs. Ashmore's in the Warrington Police Court, that also was without foundation. The jury after a brief consultation ,found the prisoner guilty of obtaining £6O by false pretenees from William Stockton. The Chairman then read the list of previous convictions against the prisoner, and passed upon him a sentence of twelve calendar months' imprisonment , with hard labour.

PARR'S BA.NK, LIMITED.

DIRECTORS' REPORT.

An extraordinary general meeting of this bank will be held at the Bank Premises, Warrington, on Thursday next. The directors in their report state that the posi- tion and prospects of the business continue to he in every way satisfactory. The amalgamation of the Con- solidated Bank, Limited, with this Company has been sucoessfully carried out. The subscribed capital has t.w.n inereased to £6,600,000 ; the naid-tin capital to £1,320,000; and the reserve fund to £1,320,000 After effecting this and providing amply for the costs of the amalgamation, there remained surplus we're of the Consolidated Bank, Limited, amounting to £206,285,, which the Directors have applied to write down consols and bank premises account, and still further to strengthen the position of the bank. Including £31.046 10s. 91., brought forward at 31qt December. 1895, by this bank, and £4.988 194 34. brought forward at the same date by the Consolidated Bank. Limited, the net balance of profits, after provid- ing for all bad and doubtful debts, ani rebating the current bills at 5 per cent.. is £172,386 141. 9i. From this sum the directors recommend the payment of a dividend at the rate of 19 per cent, per annum. free of income tax, for the quarter ended 30th June, 1896.t0 he payable on the Bth August, £62 700; dividend at the same rate for the quarter ending 30th September, 1896, to be payable on 2nd November,lB96, to the sharehnlders whose names shall be on the register on 15th October. £62 700 ; to bank premises account, £10,000; total £135,400; leaving balance to be carried to next account, £36,988 14s. 9d. Siih-branches of the bank have been opened at. Alsager (Cheshire), Cobham (Surrey), Cres- sington (Lancs.). and in the Manchester district at Weaste, and Docks, Regent-road. In conformity with the authority given by special resolution passed on 19th May, and confirmed on llth June, the directors have elected Mr. James Alex. Bannerman, Mr. James Tulloch, Mr. Alfred Crewdson, Sir John James Harwood, Mr. Emil Heinemann and Mr. Robert Nitl, inn., who were formerly directors of the Conso/idated 114nk Limited, to be directors of this bank, cod the shareholders will be asked to confirm these 4ppointrnenta.

STOCKTON HEATH.

ST. Mr. Curmah, the Workhouse Master, was in charge, and several members of the Board of Guardians also accompanied the trippers. WIDNES ATHLETIC CLUB SPORTS.

The two days meeting held annually under the aus- pices of this club, took place on the ground of the Wid- nes Football Club on Friday and Saturday. Friday evening's sport was of an interesting nature, and very enjoyable to the spectators present. The most interest- ing events were the local half mile handicap, which Peters won cleverly from Aston,and the scholars' handi- cap which was carried off by a smart little runner named Pickering, who after winning his heat in grand style, beat J. H. Boberts (who was giving the winner 16 yards in 300) by about half a yard. W. Collin, of War- rington, managed to get in first in the two miles bicycle handicap, while P. Connor, of Widnes, won a popular victory in the mile handicap, by five yards from Bouch who was alsa considered a favourite for the event. Saturday's proceedings of course, were the chief feature of the annual festivity, and the afternoon's sports was attended by many of the most prominent Widnes people, and also by a large concourse, both from home and aoroad. H. B. Msnick, of the Liverpool Wheelers, se- cured the mile bisycle handicap, and the three miles bicycle race was won by W.Lows ,of Rainhill,Monck com- ing in second a few feet behind. SUPPOSED ATTEMPTED SUICIDE NEAR

RUNCORN.

CURIOUS STORY. .. - ...........

On Tuesday night about six o'clock a fa—rtiCeTlamed Lomas found a labourer named Matthew gealey, of St. Helens, with a severe wound in his throat. He was standing by Halton Bridge, near Sutton, when found. Me. Lomas had the man taken to the workhouse. Healey was preperly treated and seen by Dr. Stlby, but in con- sequence of statements made by the man the police had to be called in. Healey during Tuesday night told different stories. In one he alleges he was set upon by two men who demanded money of him. He refused to accede to their requests, and they set upon him and carried him to a plantation near by, where one held him while the other deliberately cut his throat with a clasp knife. This with other highly improbable stories caused the police to make enquiries, and their conclusion is that the man's wounds were self inflicted. This is supported by Dr. Selby who having seen the man is thoroughly of that opinion. Questioned by the place he has made several rambling statements. On being searched he was foune, to be possessi,n of 11d. some bread, a pipe, and a piece of twist tobacco. At the time he alleged the attack was made on him there were four platelayers engaged neer the spot, and any outcry w3uld have been heard by them. Healey has stated that he worked for a Mr. Rigby near St. 11,4 ens. but can give no definite address,and also says he walked from St. Helens on Tuesday. The police have ascer- tained that no suspicuous characters have been observed in the neighbourhood.

FINDING WATER BY DIVINATION.

SUCCESSFUL DISCOVERY NEAR RUNCORN. About three months since a search for water was made on tne estate of Mr. Becketz. Chadwick, J.P., at Kingsley,by a water prospector or diviner, of the name of Mullins, from near Bath. Upon the ad- vice of Mr. Mullins, Mr. Chadwick agreed with a well sinker to commence operations without the customary boring,tor the water "diviner" gave such unmistakeable evidence of his ability to discover springs, that the preliminary boring was thought to be unnecessary. The well-sinker began at once to sink a well of four feet diameter and now, at a depth of 50 to 55ft., he has come across a very strong stream, and at the time of writing there was no less than six feet of water in the well. It may be added that Mr. Mullins only used a small haz,tl rod or wand for discovering the whereabouts of , water, and that be succeeded in doing this after an expert with all the latest appliances at his com- mand had failed.

On Tuesday evening there was a large attendance at the Warrington Corporation Baths. whim the return match in the Liverpool District Water Polo 14-ague, be- tween St. Helens and Warrington, was played off. HALTON LADIES' CLUB ANNIVERSARY

THE 80Tx CELEBRATION.

Monday saw the 80th celebration of the anniversary of the Halton Female Friendly Society. The customary arrangements were made aud about one o'clock the members commenced to gather in the schoolroom. The annual subscriptions being paid they proceeded to the Castle Hotel Green, and baying walked round it three times in accordance with the ancient custom proceeded to the Parish Church. In church an apprepria.e ser- mon was preached by the Rev. Charles Wray, of Aston. At the conclusion of the service the procession was again made up and traversed the village, headed by the Wes- ton brass band. It was preceded by Lady Brooke, Mrs. Wray, Mrs. Charles Reynolds, Mrs. Henry Reynolds. Mr. Charles Reynolds, J.P., Mr. Ga.mon, Rev. G. D. Wray, Rev. S. Wilkinson, and Mr. Thomas Wright. The num- ber taking part in the processign would be about 250. On the return to the green of the Castle Hotel an excellent tea was:served in the marquee. The statement of accounts for the last year showed that the members' subscriptions realised £l4B 185., and that there bad been paid through sickness £lll 65., funerals £6O, and surgeons' accounts £35 6s. The total funds of the society amounted to £1,461 ss. 11d., which is chiefly invested in the Manchester Sheffield, and Lin- colnshire Railway preference stock,the Runcorn Savings Bank, Parrs Bank, and the Halton Burial Board. The Rev. G. D. Wray after tea proposed a vote of thanks to Lady Brooke who was present both at the pro- cession and service. He was sure the ceremony was en- joyed more when Lady Brooke was present and they would join with him in thanking her for the kind inter- est she had taken in the society and in wishing that she might long be able to remain the patroness of the society. Lady Brooke in responding mid that since the last anniversary they had lost a good friend by the retire- ment of Mr, Charles Reynolds who for 25 years had been a trustee of the society. During that time he had worked very hard for the prosperity of the society and he had. they would admit, earned a well deserved rest. (Hear, hear.) When Mr. Reynolds first became a trustee the Society was not in the position it was at the present day. They were now able to show a good balance in the bank and they were able to meet all demands made upon them. This she considered had not be achieved without a great deal of work on the part of Mr. Reynolds who throughout his connection with the club had earned the best regards of all and had brought about the happiest results. Personally, she had a great deal to thank him for as he explained all matters touching the working of the club to her, on her becoming its patroness. She had therefore great pleasure in asking him on behalf of the officers to accept a small present as a token of their respect and esteem. (Applause.) Mr. Reynolds was then presente4 with a silver cigar- ette case and match box, in a case which bore the in- scription :—Presented to Charles Reynolds by the officers and members of the Halton Female Friendly Society, as , a small token a appreciation of his valuable services the society fir a period of 20 years. Mr. Reynolds inlreturning thanks said that no one could think how pleased he was with the beautiful present he had just received, and also with the kindly reference which had been made to his connection with the club. He did not remember haying any presents since his wedding 30 years ago. (Laughter.) The present he had received, came from the people of the place wherein he was born, and he had noticed that afternoon as they walked the green that many of the faces were familiar to him. He had retired because he felt too old, and thought that he had not sufficient brains to carry on the work of the Society in a proper manner. (Laugh- ter.) If the duties of the society were per- formed in an efficient way the club would have a grand success. Mr. Wright and Mr. Gamon had done a great deal of hard work for the bene- fit of the club and he hoped they would continue to de so. ISte was very pleased to know that Mr. Wright had taken his (the speaker's) place as trustee, for there was no man in the district who knew the routine of the club better than Mr. Wright. Be had been asked to p iint out that about 20 years ago the S )ciety had £750 invested, while at the present day it had double that amount. (Applause.) There was one thing they would have to keep their fineures on, and that was the sick pay of the deb, because if they went on at the present I heavy rate they would be getting into trouble with the Registrar at London. There had been many diseases during the last twe years such as influenza, but he thought they would fly away as quickly as they came. Delieved that day the Management Committee had received £lO from those present, by the teas thy bad been provided with. C including he thanked all present for the beautiful gift and for the kind reference they had made to him. (Applause.) Mr. J. H. GAMON said he was very pleased to see so many present, including Lady Brooke, but was very sorry his friend Mr. Reynolds ead resigned. During the time they had worked together they had always got along very comfortably. Mr. Wright endorsed the foregoing observations and proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Reynolds and Mrs. Wray, the Auditor (Mr. G. J. Knight), and surgeons (Drs. J. Selby, W. H. Cal ruthers, G. H. Ormesby, A. McLennan and J. Robinson). This was seconded and carried unanimously. The company then adjourned to the green where a band played 'during the evening for dancing. After indulging in this pleasant fashion for some time the public were given access to the grounds. A good num- ber visited the village during the afternoon and the pro- ceedings throughout were of a thoroughly enjoyable character.

PAINFUL DROWNING CASE AT

It UN CORN.

About noon on Monday, a battling fatality occurred in the Barn Pits Dear Halton Lodge. A young man named Albert Simmans a son of William Simmans of 83, Spring Gardens Runcorn in company with several others was bathing in the pits, and it is thought that he got out of his depth. He was seen to be in trouble by his com- panions who attempted to save him but unfo; tunately without success. The body was afterwards recovered and the police were informed and conveyed the deceased home on the ambulance.

INQUEI.

INTBRESTING REMARKS ON SWIMMING BY THE CORONER.

Mr. Worsley held an ingeest on Wednesday morning at the Runcorn Police Station on the body of Albert Simmans who met his death by drowning on Monday at noon. Mary Ann Simmons, wife of William Simmons, labourer, said deceased was her step-son and was a farm labourer, aged 19 next month. He left home in good health on Monday morning but did not say anything about going to bathe. Re could not swim. David Drury was a friend of the deceased and they worked togetber.—David Dr try, a youth, residing in Spring Gardens, said he was a farm labourer at the same place as deceased. The deceased, higisAf (witness) and a boy named Done went to bathe after dinner in one of Mr. Cross's fielde in Halton. He could swim himself but Done and deceased could not. They had been in the water about ten minutes when deceased took a dive and was trying to" bottom it out." Deceased got his foot into a hole but got out of it once and must have g(..t out of his depth. A boy said that deceesed was drowning and be (witness) went to his assistance. Deceased caught him by the shoulder and pullrd him down twice. He afterwards let go and went down again and he (witness) again tried to save him but it was of no use. There woe three or four boys on the bank, but they were all younger than Hinge/4 and conic). not wim.There were no men about except one who was at the bottem of the field. They called to him but when he got up deceased was drowned. The police were sent for. The deceased had just gone down for the last time when the man came.—The Coroner : What sort of a pond is it, shellow at one end and deep at the other.— Witness: It goes down when you get towards the middle.—The Coroner: Did you know that a boy was drowned there last year.—Witness: No, sir.- -rhe Coroner : Does anybody interfere with you when bathing there P—Witness : Yea, the farmer has turned boys back again.—Thl Coroner: (hen you have no rieht there.—Witness : No, e r. P.C. E lison said he was called to the pond and hel found several boys round it and there was an old man named Thomas Stekes who was lame and could not ren- der assistance. He recovered the body which was in the water about an hour. The boys, in the field worked for Mr. Grice and the pond was in the middle of it. It was shout six yards deep and ran down very suddenlv. No one was drowned in the same pond last year though a boy was drowtrd in one close by.

The Coroner said the only verdict they could return was thAt deceased was accidentally drowned while bath- ing. It was a pity boys could not learn to swim. If the lad had been able to swim his life would have been vored. It was risky for boys to go into deep ponds while not being able to swim. especially so when they went in with bo:s who could swim. They saw the h-vs who could swim going into deep water and they lotto% ed and the inevitable result was they were drov od.—A verdict was returned to the effect that de- ce..fied was accidentaliy drowned while bathing.
