IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday Mr. BRYCE asked the Under Foreign Secretary whether the Government viewed with favour and would support a settlement of the affairs of Crete which should place the rights of the Assembly and the arrangements for the local autonomy of the island under the guarantee of the Great Powers of Europe. He did not think it would facilitate the un- animity of the Powers if he made any statement of the views of the Government. Mr. BRYCE asked when he would be able to make a statement. Mr.ConzoN replied that he made one last week, and that he saw no neces- sity to repeat it.

Str.Scorr asked the Colonial Secretary if his attention had been drawn to the notice reported as having been promulgated at Capetown decreeing outlawry against the insurgents in Rhodesia,and declaring that all natives remaining under arms should suffer the punishment of death: whether he could state by whose authority it was issued ; and whether it was to be taken as supersed- ing the decree of clemency recently issued by Sir F.Car- rington. In reply, Mr.CHAmniceLAIN said the rep,rt re- ferred to was inaccurate. Be had not yet received the exact text of the proclamation, but alter stating that natives bearing arms or aiding those with arms against the lawful authorities were liable to be apprehended as rebels, and upon trial and conviction might be sentenced to death or lesser punishment, it went on to say that in order to secure protection from those penalties they must surrender themselves immedi- ately, and that those who did so before the 10,h of August would not be proceeded against, unless they were ringleaders or had been concerned in any murder or outrage.

In reply to a question put by Mr. J. H. Roberts, Mr. CURZON read a telegram from Sir H. Kitchener stating that up to July 12 there had been 705 rases f-,f cholera on the Egyptian frontier and 479 deatt;s. During the two preceding days no case had occurred at Kosheh cr Akasheh, and it was hoped that the precautions tAken might prove successful in preventing any further spread of the disease.

On the order of the day for the second reading of the Agricultural Rates, Congested Districts, and Bore) Land Tax Relief (3cetland) Bill, Sir H. CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN moved as an amendment—" That this ti,use is of opinion that the money to be allocated to Scot! ind under this bill ought net to be applied almost exc:ukively tn the benefit of one class only of the people of Scotland." At midnight the debate was adjourned.

WEDNESDAY. In the House of Commons on Wednesday Sir William Harcourt subsequently obtained leave to move the ad- journment in order to call attention to the present posi- tion of public business. The practical question he wanted to ask was what the Government intended to do with reference to the Irish Land Bill. All the information they had at present was that four days were to be de- voted to the committee on tbe bill, and they ought, to have a declaration from the Government of how the Government intended to dispose of the remaining time before the prorogation. Mr. Cxamaximant, in the course of his reply, said there was good reason to believe that the work the Govern- ment had set themselves to do—he was not now speak- ing of the Irish Land Bill—would be accomplished by the time which had always been named as the probable ter- mination of the session. In the course of further remarks Mr. Chamberlain cbarrd Mr. Dillon with inconsistency, and said that if the bill had to be withdrawn the respon- sibility would rest with him. Consideration of the Finance Bill, as amended, was re- Burned. Mr.W.Jones's amendment to omit the 31st clause, by which it was proposed to remit so much of the land tax aa exceeded in any parish, is. in the pound, was re- jected by a majority of 155, and the bill passed the re- port stage, and was set down for third reading on Mon- day.

THURSDAY.

In the House of Commons on Thursday, Sir U. Camp. BELL-BANNERMAN asked whether, in view of the judg- ment pronounced on Tuesday night by the representa- tives of Scotland upon the scheme proposed in the Scotch Rating Bill, 33 Scotch members having opposed it, while 24 supported it, the Government intended te proceed with the bill. Mr. BALFOUR replied that the principle underlying the question was to him an entirely novel one, and not one which he thoug!,t could be accepted. The House went into Commit:ee on the Irish Land Bill, resuming the consideration of clause 2, which ex- ceptedl certain holdings froir the provisions of the Irish Land Acts. Mr. M. HF.ALY moved to omit sub-section 3, which excepted from the benefits of the Land Acts town parks which adjoined a city and bore an increased value as accommodation land from ordinary holdings. Mr. G. BALFOUR accepted the amendment, which was carried by 261 votes to 122. Mr. Sairrx-BAnnv moved the omission of a sub-section of the same clause dealing with pasture holdings. The amendment was rejected by 293 votes to 54. Mr. CARSON moved that if a holding was divided into two parts the tenant should not have the power, unless with the consent of the landlord, to , surrender one portion only of the property. The amend- ment as rejected, and the second and third clauses were added to the bill.

In the House of Lords the EARL of ONsLow moved a resolution, similar to that recently passed in the House of Commons, that the ordinary pay of the Indian troops employed in the Soudan should be charged on the re- sources of India. Lord Salisbury remarked that the sum in question was mall, but it was a matter of prin- ciple. The question was the defence of Suakin). If Suakim had not been defended by the Indian taxpayers to the very small extent to which they were called into contribution, it must have been defended by the British taxpayers. Suakim meant the whole cf the southern littoral of the Red Sea, and did they mean to tell him that India had no interest in that ? Lord Rosebery de- fied anybody to connect the expedition against the dervishes with the in'erests of India, and he never had more satisfaction in his life in voting against a resolu- tion than he had in voting against that. The House divided, when there were—For the resolution, 92 against, 40; majority f, r. 52.

Mr. F. A. Lake pro- secuted on behalf of the District Council who lodged the ceera- plaint. William Garrett market inspector. prow d seeing the defendants hawking strawberries in Greenway-road, and Irwell-lane. They were not licensed hawkers and had not, paid any tolls. They had come from Oscroft. and Riley* father came last year to the market and had paid tolls to him. —The defendants pleaded that they had not been to Runcorn before and William Riley defendant Riley's fathce gave evid- ence to that effect. They did not know the market inspector or they would have paid the tolls. Defendants were fined 6d. each and 10s 6d. costs. Lately my old friend Rowton called on me. `.He had been a long time in the Colonies. •Of Course he told me about his adventures. He had had several narrow es- capes from death, and when I congratulated him, he laughed and said, "Yes, I've had one or two shaves, but never enough to get really scared." "Cuing," i answered, "what about that cave you were in in North Q ieenslan& when you didn't dare too ee foe fear. of the •arekes inside, or leave it because of the black fellows waiting to eat you outside ?" "That was bad," he re- plied. "but it was only for two days and I never quite lost bope. Now, I came across a much worse case.where a man was face to face with death, not for two days., nor twenty, but for over a hundred. Fancy death sit- ting, lying, eating, drinking and sleeping with you for moeths ;no respite from anxiety, no chance of forget- ting your doom, no fleshes of hope, no rush of action or use for energy, only a constant despairing attempt to hope foredoomed to fail I. Ah, old chap, that is torture that can break the courage of the proudest and most self-reliant." "An awful picture,'? I said, WARRINGTON BOROUGH POLICE COURT. but of course it's all imaginary." MOST READERS

Have had some experience of sick rooms, either as patients or attendants. How many such are there who have not felt the weakness or debility which follows the sickness, more discouraging than even the weakness it- self? Though the disease may have given way before the timely application of proper remedies, and though,in consequence, no immediate danger is to beapprehended, the slow progress towards convalescence is very dis- heartening to patient and attendant alike.

Hew often do we hear people say :—" Yes. The fever ,has left him. He is free from any immediate danger. •Bull he is so weak. If only he could regain strength ! " This debilitating effect of fever and other diseases is 'indeed a great source of anxiety to medical men as well as to their patients; and anything which would tend sdirectly to shorten the period between the departure of the fever and the complete recovery of strength would be naturally welcomed by both doctors and patients. This is one of the reasons why Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters is so popular, why doctors prescribe it so largely, why chemists sell it so generally, why patients have re- course to it so readily, and why everybody has a good word to say for it. Not only does it attack the disease in its stronghold, not only does it drive the evil from the system, but it gives back directly the strength of which the system has been deprived by illness; it infuses new life into the blood, and the recovery of health and strength, instead of being, as it otherwise would be, dis- couragingly slow, intermittent, and uncertain, becomes, when this excellent medicine is resorted to.encouragingly rapid, regular, and certain. It is therefore confidently recommended as an unrivalled tonic medicine, and may be safely used by all who suffer from any form of weak- ness or debility from whatever cause it may arise. Mr. 3. A Poacaxa •IN TROUBLE.—George Sephton, of 4, Back Stamford-street, made his 19th Appearance before the magis- trates and was, charged with having on the Bth July, when searched,by the polico.been in poJsession of nets and pegs for the purpose of catching game, and also in possession of two rabbits.—Defenriant pleaded guilty, but banded to the magis- trates a letter signed by a Mr. Birchall giving the defe..dant permission to catch game on his land.—P 0. Corbett stated that on the previous Wednesday morning he was on duty in Longford-street in company with P.C. Lewis when they saw the defendant coming from the direc- tion of Orford. He appeared to be very bulky about the pookets, and witness stopped him. On searching him the net and pegs produced and also two rabbits were found in his possession. It was about a quarter to six in the morning when they saw him.—Defendant stated that the °Matra did not ask him any questions or he would have told them that he had permission to take the game. The rabbits had done so much harm to the farm that he had got permission to take what he could catch.—The Chief Con- stable said the detendtnt was a well-known poacher, and a very wary man. Mr. Harrison: He has got his wits about isim.—Mr. Monks: You cannot blame him for that.—The Mayor: He may have permission to catch game from a farmer with one acre, and then go to other places.—Mr. Monks stated that he was in favour of a remand for a week in order that enquiries might be made.—The Chief Constable said the defendant never did any work.—The magistrates ul- timately agreed to fine the defendand ss, and costs or in de- fault, seven dlys' imprisonment, and the nets, Ice., to be forfeited.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

AN UNDUTIFUL SON. Before the magistrates at the Warrington Borough Court on Monday, John William Travis, a young man of bad character,was charged with stealing from the dwell- ing house of his father, George Travis, Winwick-road, a man's shirt value 2s. his property,—Prisoner when asked if he would be tried here or at the Liverpool Sessions • said he would ge for trial to Liverpool.—The Chief Con- stable said that on Thursday, the 9th inst., George Trav:e retired to bed leaving his house secure and his shirt by the fireplace in the hack kitchen. Prisoner had been driven from home owing to his bad conduct, and about 430 a.m. his parents were aroused by a peculiar noise outside. George Travis looked out and observed prisoner in the act of poking a prop through the back kitchen window. He disap- peared over the wall, and the father then discovered that several of the windows were broken and the shirt gone. He gave information to the police and prisoner was appmhended. He was more than suspected of being concerned in one or two patty burglaries in the neighbourhood of Warrington. The Chief Constable applied for a week's remand, which was granted. ASSAULT ON RAILWAY OFFICIALS. On Moneay at the Warrington Borough Court, before the Mayor (chairman), Mr. F. Monks and Alderman, Harrison, John Dooley and Thomas Rafferty,two Earles- town colliers, were charged with assaulting ticket col- lector Bennett at Bank Quay station on Saturday night. Rafferty was also charged with assaulting Detective Charles Henry Parsons, at the same time and place.— Detective Parsons said that On Saturday night at 10.35 the two prisoners came to Bank Quay Station and asked for the Earlestown train, and Bennett told them it had gone. They refused to believe him and Bennett gave them platform tickets. They went up to the platform and then came down and abused Bennett. They refused to give up their tickets and he tried to detain them. They struck him and witness then went to his assistance and got the tickets from them. Defendants refused to go and Rafferty struck witness in the mouth and made several attempts to bite him—Bennett, the collector corroborated and defendants were fined 103. and costs each.

NOTES AND NEWS.

A European ship owning association has says "Science Siftings ' placed an order for the construction of a large steamer,which is to be sp-cially aiapted and fitted for the accommodation of inyali.,s in need of fresh air and a favourable climate. The t,h,p is to be in constant em- ployment for eight or nine mouths in the year,but for at least three months out of twelve she will go into dock, in order to be thoroughly cleansed and disinfected. It is proposed to make this steamer, which is the fore- runner of a large fleet ai:nilarly ( quipped, a veritable Boating palace in the matters of both comfort and salu- brity.

A correspondent of the "Daily Chronicle " writes : There is one CUTiOUEf teature about the present position of the coal trade which will make it extremely awkward for the coalowners to insist upon a reduction of wages just now. All of them, with scarcely an exception, sup- ported the Tory party last July, and they begged of their men to believe that " Unionism " meant better trade and higher wages. Mr. Ratcliffe Elis, the mine owner's secretary on the Conmliation Board, was Colonel Blundell'S election agent, and he and the Member for Ince succeeded in turning out Mr. Sam Woods on that very cry. Yet twelve months of Unionist government has brought about a cry for a ten per cent. reduction of miners' wages.

The members of Parnament who took their trial trip in the autocar that careered round Palace-yard do not come plain either of the smell or the noise. Their chief com- plaint relates to- the vibration, which some of them.eay was consideiabFe,while others say it did not exceed that of a railway carriage. In the ca:;ef of the car in questiOn the motive power was derived from . benValine, and it is at least satisfactory that its perfume did' not arreo attention ; but ae I know, the noise it made from the rapid clinking of .the machinery was considerable, and this is one of the points inventors will have to look to. The cost of the benaeline coniumAd is ridiculously small,. being only a halfpenny per mlie,4nd the vehicleroareered about with the ease or a circus hor4e.

Am amusing incident is said by a Brussels correspon- dent to have occurred at the It• yal dinner given to Li Hung Chang on Thursday evening. After passing into the state drawing-roonvat the conclusion lf the dinner, the Chinese envoy, apuarently in ignoronce of the rule of Court etiquette which strictly trvbids smoking in the State apartments, pulled out a long pipe, which, duly filled and lighted by his secretary, was returned bi the owner, who began drawing at it with evident satisfac- tion to himself. lijng:Leopold, witty kindly tact,. had cigarettes thereupon passed round,and in a few minutes the whole company was puffing vigorously.

What -was at first belie v.ed to be a case of attempted. suicide, and reported from-Newry as such, now turns out to have been only an old man's desperate remedy for the relief of laboured breathing. James M'Kee, the man referred to, is a centenarian. For years he has been an inmate of Newry Workhouse. Being afflicted with asthmatic bronchitis, on Friday he carried into execution the idea of puncturing his body with a cob— bler's knife in order, as he says himself, " to let out the wind." On a previous occason, it now appears, he per -- formed a similar operation, arid strange to relate, actu— ally experienced some relies in,hr,-athing in consequence. This time, however, it is feare,l that the knife went to deep, and that the great loss of blood in the case of a man in his advanced years will) prove fatal.

The New York correspondent of the " Standard writes :—Comments are made by. the " Herald," " Times," " Press," and " Tri hurtle" upon the cordiality with which the Bostonian Artiliert men have been re- ceived in London ; And they express the view that it pos- sesses exceptional significance. The " Tribune " resents as unworthy, both of England and the United States, the idea that England could need or desire, or that the United States could accept, any sort of alliance based upon any exigency, of European politics. The true motive is the essential unity between England and the United States. Mr-. Samuel Plimsoll will sail for England on Tuesday,after obtaining confirmation of his idea that hostility to Ecgland is fostered by American school-book' history. He has books and testimony to prove it, and has a promise from/ the Federal Commissioners of Educa- tion to discuss thEy subject in his next report. Mr. Plim- soll has hundreds of rresa clip,)lng3 upon his errand, and scores-of private letter:, winch in detail show that his errand was fruitful almost before the seed was planted.

Hp has been three times to America, but, as he always arrived in the summer, he has had no experience ef the social life of N w York,and knows only Newport and Bar Harbour. 7Lettero to the 0-Ditor.

OLD AGE PENSIONS AS A SUPPLEMENT TO

WAGES.

Sir,—l look upon this scheme for giving the working man a pension at the age of 60 or 65 as a simple act of justice already too long delayed. At present it is in much the same hopeful condition as the famous scheme for "belling the cat." Both schemes are good, but there seems a little difficulty in carrying them out. There is no one to put on the bell and there is to one to pay the pension. Mr. Chamberlain and others most ingeniously propose that the workingman pay a part of the money. What a good and benevolent idea! How very rich it sounds. Dear workingman, you have worked very hard, so hard that you have made England the richest country under the sun and there is not another like her. Thanks to your life long labours, for deny it who can, there is none that can work like an Englishman.' Therefore we offer you our best advice—(very handsome present this) —our best advice mark you, which is," that you become thrifty ; that is to say do with a little less food,and don't drink so much,and—be careful with regard to the pur- chase of fine clothes and costly furniture. Yes,we know that your wife has had only one new dress since 3 our marriage,that little Bob wants books,that Polly is in need of a new frock,that there is only si.in the house towards paying this quarter's rent now nearly due, and that you are nearly distracted because you don't know how to get the money to enable you to live; but never say die, be determined to save a bit of money, keep on saving for forty-five years or so and we will try to get Government or somebody to add to your savings and thus give you a pension for the remainder of your life, if you live to be sixty-five." This then is the present state of things— there is nothing left for honourable old age but the ten- der mercies of the dishenourable workhouse. This is to be deplored, everybody says it's a shame. The Select Committee which sat in 1885-86-87 were of the same opinion, yet they could not say how old age was to be provided with a pension. Where is the diffi- culty ? Well, I must say that it seems at first sight to be a money difficulty. lam of another way of thinking. Supposing money in England was multiplied by any figure in the multiplication table,would such an increase of money solve the problem? Not a bit! Who would think of providing the workingman with a pension ? It is true some would think about it as many have already, and even speak about it. But thinking and speaking are not in themselves sufficient to provide a pension fund. Were they, there would have been but little cause for me to write this. There is plenty of money in England but the reason for its application to the purposes of granting the workingman an old age pension has never been made out. To say he ought not to be sent to the workhouse is not a reason why he should have a pen- sion, nos would I give him a pension because it would be kind and charitable to do ; for this would pauperize him; but if I can plainly show that he has earned a pension above and beyond the money he has received as wages—if I can show that a pension would he simply money" for value received " end that consequently, he cannot be deprived of it hc eestly—then I thiuk that England will pay this pension in accordance with her traditional love of justice. I find that the workingmen of England earn collec- tively £500,000,000 per annum (Griffin). It this vast inconceivable SUM be the amount of wages astuelly Reid it is very much under-stated. For notice—if you em- ploy a Bien and agree to pay him £1 per week, that man is bound to earn more than a pound or you will certainly discharge him for another more capable. You see if a man only earns his wages he is an unprofitable servant and therefore an impossible one. It is beyond question an employe must be a source of profit; how enuch,depends on circumstances, but say anything from ten to three hundred per cent. There is no need of straining and I am content by stating the lesser sum as the value of work done for doing whieh the working- man has really received nothing. This may seem un- warrantable. Let me explain. You employ a man at stated wages, say at £1 per week. At the same time you wish to make a profit. You have got him as cheaply as possible in order to pro- fit as much as possible. Then if you realise a profit the man must earn more than a pound. And if he earn more thau a pound he does work for which he gets nothing. The fact is all earnings over a pound rightly belong to the employer fio matter how much the man may do he gets his pound awl according to custom and law he is paid, and there is an end to the matter. I wish to point out that the working man gives more than he receives and so far as I know there is no way to prevent him from doing so even if it were desirable. For whe would be bothered with men if 'there was nothing to begot out of them, so that it is a standing rule that a master shall get the greatest amount of work done for the smallest amount of wages. In this manner the work- ing man is kept poor whilst he is always making his country rich. If a man whose wages are twenty shillings per week earn 33. 81. per day six days per week for fifty weeks a year,he will earn for his employer £5 for which he gets nothing; which is exactly ten per cent.on a year's wages. It is very rarely so small a sum as ten per cent is ob- tained on wages—generally it is much more. But this small sum will amount to £2OO in forty years. This is not all when he receives his 20s. he is bound to spend them ; and no matter how careful he may be in the manner of his spending, he will not receive the full value of his money according to wholesale prices ; for each one of the dealers he buys from will not forget to take some profit from him. In the first place his wages are less than his earnings—because he has to helo to keep his employer. In the second place the commodities he buys are of less marketable value than his waees—be- cause he has to help to keep the shopkeepers. But it may be asked has he to pay more for what he buys than others pay ? Certainly. Accidents, personal and family afflic- tions, shortness of work and no work at all, too often compel him to fall into debt aod to keep a shop book. He is now tied to deal at a particular shop for the necessary things of life, agreeing to pay on Saturday for goods bought in the week, if the shopkeeper has any religion at all it is the religion of Self. His faith is sure and steadfast in number one, be cannot afford to give any- thing. Net even does he give credit. Ne, he sells it, and he soils it at his own price which the werking-man is bound to pay. If we pursue this theme a little further we shall fad that in addition to keeping his employer and shop keeper lie may have to contribute towards the maintenance of a host of County Court ofileials, from the Judge downwards. In this way he generally "pave through the nose" for the goods he buys. If we aek the reason for these things, the smallness of his wages will fairly account for them. The man who buys by the ton buys cheaply, vehilst the man who goes to market with sixpence buys most dearly. Ii this way the working-man pays the ,e,weatest price. Plainly poverty is the most expensive thing in this world, and I estimate that the credit that the work- ingman gets for the small quantities of provisions he - buys will cost him ten per cent. over and above the price of the goode. I submit that it cannot be denied that in working he benefits his employer to the extent of two shillings per week Ale in epnding his twenty shillinen the shop- . s e keeper clears aneth ,r two ehillinge out of him. (This is eupposing that his ernpl e'er does not overwork him, nor the shop men ever-reacti him.) 'This means that he gives twenty per cent, more value to his country than he re- ceives. Indeed it is very dill-Imole to truly estimate the value of labour. Intrinsically it is of more value than 'old, and we have not yet succeeded in separating 1 ibour from the labourer. Jus' -nee I slid that the workingman collectively earned " £500.000,000 a veer." Ten per cent. on this is £5O 000,000 over and ;lbw. the amoinit ef wages he re- ceives. Truly wages, are not an equivalent for work done.

Here we make a mistake. Nominally his wages are £500,000,000. Really these are not his wages that he works for. Oh no! A man's wages are the things that he gets in exchange for the money he receives, such as his food, his drink, his clothes, his goods ; in short, his living. In making this exchange he receives ten per cent. less than his nominal wages which goes as profit Ls the dealers be buys from. By these means he adds a second fifty-millions to the national wealth, or in other words he receives one hundred millions a year less than be has earned. In the face of these faets,can we say that the working man has not already earned a pension? Most truly he has ! Then in the name of fair play let him have it. That wages are not equal to work has been acknow- ledged long ago. I well remember when they were sup- plemented by parish pay. Indeed, the provisions of the poor law is a case in proof. also in the case of the statesman, the soldier, and many others who, having served their country well, a grateful country has added to their wages, stipend or salary, a pension when their work hall been done. Let me ask, who has served his country so long, so sincerely, so successfully as the ' workingman? and that amid countless dangers, which too often prove fatal. Most truly he is the feundation of his country and the fountain of her wealth. But what a reward is his—poor fare, poor clothes, and poor shelter! Yet without him England could not exist. In conclusion, as it is utterly impossible for wages to equal work in value, England is greatly indebted to the workingman. And because he has worked and not been fully paid for his work, England owes him a living, which, to be honest, must be paid. But, oh sir, this living is not imprisonment for life in a work- house ;to be in the custody of a jailor called a work- house master; to submit to rules that grind his manhood to pauper-dust; to rob him of his liberty, his love, his life-long associations ! ! This curse is not the living England should mete out to those who have been enrich- ing her for forty long years with their valuable laboura. No,sir. But instead,let the aged workman (he or she as the case may be) live in his own cottage, smoke his own pipe, road his own book, in company with his own wife, in short,sit under his own apple or pear tree " none dar- ing to make him afraid."—Yours truly, FELIX SUMMERS. 5, Walton-road, Stocaon Heath.

===== 0002601_18960718_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINCiTON MUNICIPAL FINANCE.

A CRITICISII.

INTERESTING INTERVIEW.

Our readers will remember a series of articles on Municipal Finance which appeared in this journal a little more than two years ago. With the object of ascertaining whether in the opinion of the writer of those articles any progress had been made in regard to the finances of the Borough he was in- terviewed by an " Examiner' reporter, this week. We may add that this gentleman is connected with a large manufacturing firm in the town, and besides being an expert financier, has had some experience in municipal affairs. Our reporter took with him a copy of the Borough Treasurer's recently published abstract of accounts for the year ending 31st March 1896, and asked him for a few observations thereon. For some time the gentleman in question was closely occupied in studying the voluminous ac- counts. At length throwing the book on the table, and reclining in his chair, he said "A more confused statement of accounts I think it was never the lot of man to study or peruse! Why cannot the ratepayers have placed before them a clear statement showing the actual amount of the Borough debt; the amount paid in interest each year; and the amount paid in the reduction of the principal together with a total list of assets? I made the same sugges- tions two years ago but no improvement has been made. Just look at this footnote on page 155, stating that against the Borough Debt, the Cor- poration owns Gas Works, Water Works, Town Hall and Park 'and the rest of it. I know they own these but what are they worth to-day ? What amount of depreciation has taken place, and what is their true value ? "I suppose you would be told" said the reporter, " that in the case of such an undertaking as the Water Works, no depre- ciation has taken place, forl understand that they have spent an amount on new mains every year since it came into their possession. But what do you think of the rather remarkable increase in the assessable value of property in the Borough ? "Well, 1 am glad to note that there is an increase of £lB,OOO since 1893. I pointed out the under-assessment of Warrington in your issue of April, 28, 1894, when I made some observations on the borough accounts of the previous year. There has been a great leap made in the rateable value since. During the years 1891-2-3 i; had remained stationary or nearly so at £150,000. This year it is £168,660." "The rates have been reduced since 1893?" "Yes, this increased assessment has enabled them to reduce the rates by 81. in the _E. Credit for this must be given to the Assessment Committtee, an not to any shrewdness on the part of the Finance Committee in reborrowing money at a low rate of interest."

_ "Your statement of two years ago that the borough was under-assessed, viewed in the light of the figures presented now in the Corporation ac- counts, seems to have been correct? The new property that has been built would not have in- creased the rateable value by that amount in three years?" "The Chairman of the Finance Committee con- tradicted my statement at the time, and contended that at £150,000 the town was assessed upon its letting value! But if that was so, how is it that it has increased £IS,OOO in the short space of three years? How comes it about that they have been able to decrease the rates by Bd. in the £? They have done as much in three years in the way of in- creasing the assessable value as previously was done in six !" "1 think you previously suggested that the Cor- poration should consolidate its debts, get the time extended for the loans to run,and obtain the money at a lower rate of interest? Has your suggestion been carried out in any way ?" "There is no evidence in the accounts before me that any of the loans have been consolidated. I pointed out (here referring to his newspaper cut- ting book) on the 14th April, 1894, that by consoli- dating the borough debt a saving of something like £3,000 per annum could be effected or equal to 51. in the £ on the rates." "And what was to be said against that ?-

" It was argued that if the .Corporation obtained an Act of Parliament empowering them to consoli- date the debts,it could not over-ride the agreements entered into by the Corporation with the bond- holders. An agreement, it was said, was an agreement. But this is mere sentimental- ism. The city fathers who negotiated these early loans never anticipated the glut in the money market of to-day, and why should not the present generation take advantage of it? Let the Corpora- tion, as other bodies have done, obtain an Act to consolidate their debts, now amounting to over half a million sterling, and they could pay out these old bondholders, with their high rates of interest, without trouble. lam certain that the half-million required could be obtained at ,-or 2i. per cent. interest within twelve hours from being placed on the Stock Exchange. I should think et least 10s. per cent. interest could be saved. The Finance Committee have had a golden opportunity to refund their debt this year 9wing to the increase of the assessable value, but they have not been wise enough to take advantage of it. The writer of those lines on the Corporation which appeared in your paper some time ago,was perfectly right when he said : " There's a host of thinas to be done, The money has first to be won, The taxes red Heel The burdens reloosed, In good old Warrington."

WARRINGrON.

A LITTLE GIRL BURNED.

On Monday morning, about eleven Q'clock, a little girl, named Florence Herbert, aged three years,and who lives with her parents at Pierpoint-st., was admitted to the Warrington Infirmary suffering from burns about the body, received, it is believed, whilst playing with matches. She is now progressing favourably.

Mor- timer, the members of the above club were per- mitted to ramble through Birdswood, Preston Brook. Though the weather and locality were alike favour- able,there was only a small attendance. Those mem- bers who did go were amply repaid for their exer- tions, since, though no absolutely new records were made, about a dozen rare species of plants were noted, whilst the boxes and bottles of the entomologists were speedily leaded with a pro- fusion of good specimens BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHOIR.

On llonday, through the kindness of the trustees the above choir held their annual trip to Llan- dudno. Tha weather was everything that could be desired, and this, together with the excellent ar- rangements which had been made, contributed in no small degree to the visitors' enjoyment. In the afternoon a drive round Great Orme's Head was particularly enjoyable, and after tea, which was served at Mr. Summet's, of the Grosvenor Restaur- ant, a hearty vote of thanks tc) the trustees was pas.secl,on the motion of Mr. Harrison, organist and choirmaster. Home was reached soon after nine o'clock, everyone admitting that it was the most enjoyable trip they had ever had.

SEQUEL TO THE THUNDERSTORM. On Monday morning at half-past nine o'clock a gas explosion occurred at the house of Mrs. Webb, 9, Dutton-st. There had been a leakage of gas in the kitchen due to the late thunderstorm, and to remedy this defect she sent for the gas-fitters to examine the pipes. While the men wero busily engaged in this,when half the flags which cover the kitchen were removed from the floor, an explosion occurred. Two children and Mrs. Webb, who were sitting on the sofa, were thrown to the floor, the flags were torn up, the door leading into the street was torn off its hinges, falling upon Mrs. Webb and pinning her to the ground. Mr. Lake, one of the worktn,?n,was struck in the face violently with a piece of the debris. Happily except the small injury received by him, no one was hurt.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

CRICKET.

It is true enough that there is " nothing succeeds like success " and the Warrington cricket eleven further exemplified it on Saturday by inflicting a crushing defeat en the Cheadle Dulme team who made teir first visit. From the fact that they only put together 53 runs against Warrington's 151, I should imagine they will viea the next engagement with Warrington with something like apprehension. There was a fair attendance of specta- tors on the Arpley ground but none predicted the total collapse of the visitors after their first wicket was down. Scrimshaw was, however, at his best and took eight wickots for 23 runs, all of which succumbed to his last 6 overs, from which only 5 runs were scored, the batting being devoted to the first four. Saturday was in fact, the professionals' day for not only did Scrimshaw effect this remarkable performance, but his brother " pro. " wielded the willow in masterly style for the fine score of 72. It will be again noticed what a strange fatality attaches itself to 70, or thereabouts, for this season alone 71 has been made three times and now Thewlis has added a 72 to the list.

The visitmrs were fortunate enough at the commence- ment to win the toss and they of course prepared to ex- tract runs from the fine wicket which had been prepared. Their initial partnership was between A. E. Taylor and C. V. Rome. By the way,there were three of the visit- ing team bearing the latter pronnioing name but none of them could persuade the ball to indulge in " roaming " to any extent. Taylor batted freely and drove the bowling to the boundary repeatedly. Connor and Scrim- shaw were each driven twice in succession but the fez mer, revenged himself by sending dowu a ball which collided with Taylor's middle stump.ll6 had made 28 in very quick time but it was the first and last flash of the visiting team. C. U. ,Taylor joined Rome who was playing cautiously, and commenced by unceremoniously sending a slow one from Connor to the margin of the field. The next over however was differ- ent for Connor put in a ball which lifted Taylor's off stump out of the ground before he was quite aware that it had arrived. At this point Scrim- shaw got "set," and in oue over spread the stumps of E. Rome, C. H. Williams, and A. Rome, the first two with successive balls. C. V. Rome retired in the next over, and following this S. Colles and W. G. Redfern were also dismissed with successive balls. The bowler was thus aggravatingly near to performing the "hat trick" twice in a match. R. Todd now occupied the wicket and had the distinction of carrying out his bat for 8. Mayor was bawled by Scrimshaw for 4, and G. Colles became lbw for a "duck." This Concluded the innings, and it redounds greatly to Flampson's credit that not a single extra was allowed. Fielding was also smart, but very few chances of a catch were provided by the short stay of each of the Cheadle flulme bats- men.

Not much effort was required of Warrington to pass the score of the visitors and they succeeded in trebling it comfortably. Bibby and Thewlis opened, but the start was disappointing. Bibby was beaten by Mayor at eight and Connor retired without scoring when the runs had totalled 11. Thewlis was, however, well within his powers and being j lined by Hampson runs came rapidly. Fours fell to each batsmen, but after hitting 12 Flampson fell to Mayor. Clarke left the " pro " to do the hitting and stayed till the half century was reached, when he had the satisfaction of scoring the winning hit. A few minutes later the home captain let Mayor in and W. H. Podmore took his place. Between him and Thewlis a long partnership ensuedgand both the batsmen made the ball travel. A. E. Taylor, A. Romegand S. Colles, were put on to bowl but could not affect a separation. The century was hoisted amid cheers and Thewlis had then knocked up 50. Again the bowling was changed and Thewlis was disposed of through failing to reach the crease. His 72 had included some particularly skilful batting. Podmore continued,' but England, Voisey, Scrimshaw, and R. Silcock were all out for small scores, and Podmore carried out his bat for a level half century. A collection was taken for the two professionals, and the pleased spectators were not slow in response.

An equally decisive victory was obtained by the second eleven at Cheadle Flulme, when they put toge- ther the excellent, total of 205. Batting first they sent in Adamson and Silcock who opened well. The latter was caught and bowled for 24 and his partner made 26. Unsworth joined the Warrington captain and negotiated the boundary with freedom. An hour and a half's play saw the century put up, and three runs later Unsworth was run out, after contributirm a fine 53. Rushworth added 35, Duckworth 22, and Soh. 15 (not out). Cheadle Flulme only contrived to make 77, of which Alcock and Kirk scored 21 and 24 respectively,and were both caught by Duckwnrth. The visitors were in a catching hum- our, for six of the homesters were thus dismissed, three in succession by Duckworth. Crompton and Unsworth bowled well.

The Cheshire Lines Engineers proved victorious over St. Helens L.& N.-W. on Saturday. They both batted and bowled well. Plackett contributed 23. Walker 38, and Gleave (set out), 19. A tote of 110 was com- piled. The Saints were unable to play the bowline of Monks and Harrison at all, the former taking 6 wickets for 25 runs and performing the " hat trick," and the latter 4 wickets for 12 runs. 39 runs was all that the visitors could put against the C.L.E. figure.

The second elevens of these clubs played at St. Helens. The Engineers were beaten by 13 runs. Genders with 11 was the ocly batsman who did well, while for the homesters Wilson commenced the innings with 13. Ten extras were alsa added to the score, and these went far towards victory.

Grappenhall second was in a scoring humour on Saturday. for at Winnint_ton they made 164 runs for three wickets. The home eleven placed 77 against this, and were thus badly •` licked." Wicket-keeping was good on both sides, for only two extras went between them.

A close game was played at Moore between St.Elphins and the home eleven with the result that the Saints were beaten by 7 runs. Hattnn not out made 15,which was the highest score on either side. Moore made 39 runs and received 8 extras, 'nom than they gave. An- trobus bowled well for them, and Duckworth for the Saints.

There was an afternoon's exercise at Moore on Mon- day night, when Daresbury and Moore engaged to play a cricket match. ita the latter only made 12 runs be- tween them, and the former 7, the description "cricket match "is somewhat ot a misnomer. Eleven O's were made during the game.

CYCLING.

Saturday was quite a red-letter day in the annals of ) the Warrington Cycling club, as Mr. J.) Naylor, of Beeston Castle, had invited as many members of the club who could make it convenient, to ride and have tea with him at his beau- ful residence, and about 30 members availed themselves of his kind invitation. After a pleasant ride from War- rington to Beeston they were met at the gate by Mr. sylor, who conducted them round the various places of interest at his place, and shortly afterwards all sat down to a sumytucus repast, provided over by Mr. and Mrs. J. Naylor, in a spacioui room overlooking the grounds. After ample justice had been done to the viands. Mr. E. Pickavance, one of the hon. secretaries of the club, pro- posed a hearty vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Ntvlor for their generosity. In the course of his remarks he alluded to the fact that Mr. Nayler's cycling experi- ences dated back from the times of the " boneshaker," and he had kept up his enthusiasm to the preAent day, being one of the chief consuls for Cheshire of the Cyclist Touring Club, and doing an immense amount of good to cycling generally. Mr. D. Davies seconded the proposition in a neat little speech.in which he spoke in glowing terms of Mr. Navlor's work on behalf of cycling. The proposition be- ing carried with cheers,Mr. Naylor in a few well-chosen remarks replied.and expressed a hope that the visit of the club mif.,,nt be an annual one,a sentiment which was received with great enthusiasm. After a stroll round, an adjournment was made into the house,and a musical entertainment followed. Messrs. W. Brown, W. Meun. field, J. Pemberton, J. Naylc r, D. Clayton, and F. Cooke obliging the company with their services. The machines were then got out, and with hearty cheers for Mr. and Mrs. Naylor and family, the patty wended their way across the dusky avenues of Delamere Forest, and after a pleasant run of a couple of hours the lights of War- rington loomed up on the horizon and reminded the cyclists that the good old town Vr as reached after one of the most pleasant outings the club has ever held.

On Tuesday evening about 25 members of the club went; for a ron co Plumbley. Here the party put up at " The Smoker" and had an impromptu concert there. An enj iya.ble ride home im the evening was experienced.

SUCCESSFUL MUSIC PUPILS.

Mr. E. Wilkes, organist of St. Paul's Church, and pupil of Mr. J. J. Lythgoe, L.L.C.M., has success- fully passed the diploma examination in connec- tion with the London College of Music held at Liverpool. This success carries with it the privi- lege of appending to his name the letters A.L.C.M., also to wear the college cap and gown. Of the three examinations held in December, April, and July, he has passed all, having gained four certifi- cates and the diploma. At the recent examination held in Liverpool, in cennection with the London College of Music, the following pupils of Mr. Lyth- goe also successfully passed : Pianoforte playing (elementary) Elsie hythgoe and Bessie Clark. At the recent examination of the Royal Academy of Music (Liverpool Centre). Miss Hannah Brough, contralto, pupil of Mr. Levi Richardson, passed with honours in singing—intermediate.

FUNERAL OF CORPORAL EVANS.

With railli:a73-7-11-oM-nu7s--. the remains of Corporal Charles Evans,of Green-st., Warrington, were laid to rest in the Warrington Cemetery on Sunday afternoon, in the presence of a large concourse of people. The deceased was only 22 years of age,and was a member of the cycling corps in connection with the Warrington Volunteers. He had been connected with the Volunteers since 1891, and was promoted to the position of corporal on the 27th May last. He attended the annual encampment at Conway this year, and although his health had not been good for some time, his death came unex- pectedly. He was connected with Holy Trinity Sun- day School, and many scholars attended the funeral as a mark of respect. There was a strong muster of Volunteers in attendance. They formed up in the Drill Hall under the command of Captain R. J. Chorley at 12.15, and marched to the deceased's late residence, where a procession was formed headed by the Volunteer Band, conducted by Bandmaster Armstrong. Along the route taken by the cortege the band played at intervals the Dead March in Saul." There were 160 volunteers in the procession, and amongst the officers present were :—Sergeant-Major Sullivan. Sergeant-Instruc- tor Barker, and a number of colour-sergeants and sergeants. The service was conducted by the Rev. 11. V. Pigot, vicar of St. Paul's. The coffin was covered by the Union Jack and numerous wreathe, and was conveyed to its last resting-place by mem- bers of the cycling corps. The customary three volleys were fired over the deceased's grave. LOYAL ORDER OF ANCIENT SHEPHERDS, A.U.—

Cunliffe was remanded to the Newton Sessions, bail being allowed.

HAYDOCK COLLIERIES EXCURSION.

The annual excursion promoted for the benefit of the employes of Messrs, K. Evans and Co., of Hay- dock and Golborne took place on Saturday to Black- pool. Something like 3,000 men, women and chil- dren left Haydock in four special trains between 5.35 a.m. and 6.55, and Blackpool was reached with- out mishap in about a couple of hours afterwards. It goes without saying that all the places of inter- est were visited,and a very large number,taking ad- vantage of the balmy weather, enjoyed an annua dip in the" briny." The return journey was made at various times the first train leaving Blackpool at 6.35 p.m. and the last at 11.20 p.m.

At,the morning and evening services excel- lent sermons were preached by the Rev. Daniel R.todes, vicar of St. Gabriel's, Middleton, Oldham, and there were fairly large congregations present. PROMOTION FOR POLICE SERGEANT CLARKE (EARLESTOWN.) Police Sergeant Clarke, of Earlestown, has been appointed Inspector of Weights and Measures for the division of Ashton-under-Lyne, and he left Earlestown on Tuesday to take up his new duties. It is exactly 12 months since Sergeant Clarke was promoted from Constable and transferred from Birkdale to Earlestown. During his stay at the latter place he has gained many friends by his own never failing courtesy and kindness, and he leaves Earlestown with the good wishes of the townspeo- ple for his success. He has also carried the esteem and respect of the officers with whom he was as- sociated, and his removal from Earlestown will be viewed by them with regret. Sergeant Clarke is but a young man, and his rapid advancement is a proof of his sterling merit 7, WARGRAVE CHURCH CHOIR.

The members of the above choir,to the number of I 27,held their annual picnic on Saturday, the destin- ation this year being Llandudno. Leaving Earles- town by the 7.25 a.m. train in reserved carriages, they reached Llandudno at half-past ten, after a wait of half an hour at Chester. Dinner was served at 11.45 at the Parade liotel,and the catering being of an excellent character, the meal was much en- joyed. After dinner five landaus were chartered and the party drove to Colwyn Bay, the journey there and back and the beauty of the scenery on the route being most enjoyable. Tea was partaken of at the Parade Hotel at 5.30, after which the 114- tervening time was spent in shopping, Sze. Llan- dudno was left at 7.3o.Earlestown being reached at 10.20. The day was beautifully tine and a very pleasant outing resulted. The arrangements were carried out by Mr. E. Leyland, and were of an ex- cellent character. BURGLARY AT NEWTON.

Between Saturday and Monday evening the resi- dence of Mrs. Appleton, Park Road, Newton, was burglariously entered during the absence from home of the occupants. Mrs. Appleton left home for a holiday on Saturday leaving everything secure, and on Monday evening a neighbour, Mrs. Winstanley, went into the back yard for the purpose of feeding Mrs. Appleton's cats, when she saw that the back kitchen window had been forced, and the house entered. She saw something was wrong and wired for Mrs. Appleton who returned home on Wednes- day. An examination of the premises disclosed the fact that although all the drawers had been forced open and everything in the place had been scattered ' about, nothing was missing. All the silver and other articles of value were intact, and it is sup- posed the burglars were in search of money, of which commodity they found none. A missionary box was broken open and emptied,but it is thought there were only a few coppers in it. The police have the matter in hand. EARLESTOWN FOOTBALL CLUB.

On Mon—day evening the medals gained by the members of the above team in consequence of their winning the Lancashire Alliance Championship for the past stmson, were distributed at the Griffin Hotel by Mr. Couneillor Beatty. In connection wili the distribution a smoking concert was held at which there were about 80 persons present. Mr. Beatty presided. and in the course of the evening presented the following members of the team with the medals, to each of whom he spoke a few worth;

of encouragement, viz., Messrs. W. Wilkinson (cap- tain), Joseph Lee, J. Mather. C. Taylor, A. Limond, H. Clarke, J. Clibborn, J. Topping, H. Edin,)ndson, C. Lee, J. Lee, T. Hughes, and A. Lee. The melia:s were of gold and bore a suitable inscription. _ _ . _

Several toasts were proposed and duly honoured, and songs were contributed by the follGwing, Messrs. S. Ss% ann, J. liuddleston, W. Beatty, J. Mel- lor, J. Shaw, and J. Dee. Votes of thanks to t Chairman and singers brought the proceedings to a close.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

The proceedings of the District Council last week were remarkable for one thing, and that was their extreme brevity. To get through all the business of the Council in one hour is an accomplishment of which the members may feel proud. They seem to have put aside the little bickerings which at the formation of the Council characterised the proceed- ings, and to have settled down to a business-like attitude. Of course this is all due to the committees, at which the real work and discussion takes place, and of which the outside public know little or nothing. There is almost an entire absence in the press ot reports and comments on scenes, and per- haps it is as well this is so, as certainly it is not creditable to the Council to ficTe blazoned forth to the world a monthly account of scenes which are only looked upon by sober people as ridiculous.

While on this subject I should like to nllude to the intimation given try Councillor Gilmour that he would bring before the next committee meeting the question of the publication of the committee's minutes before the holding of the monthly Council meeting. For my part Ido not see what difference the publication of the minutes can make, nor what influence they can have upon the meeting. Thie question was brought up some time ago, and opin- ions on the policy of publication were then some- what divided, but no steps were taken to prevent the public being informed beforehand of the busi- ness to be considered at the Council, and I am not aware that anything has transpired since then to warrant the Council in requesting the non-publica- tion of the minutes. I think the Council should do all in their power to prevent anything being done which would savour of secrecy ; all their doings should be open to public criticism. I am an advo- cate for the admission of the Press to committee meetings.

The serious trap accident which happened at the Vulcan last week should act as a warning to drivers of vehicles through that dangeroue locality. It is well known that the Vulcan is reached by a rather steep brow,alongside which runs the L. & N.-W.R. and towards the bottom of the declivity two sets of rails cross. Further than this the submit is on a somewhat sharp curve, and drivers of vehic:es should exercise extreme care in driving down the hill. This is not the first accident of the kind which has happened, and yet I learn that vehicles are ofted driven down at a speed sufficient to make a timid parson's hair stand on end. Furious driving is not a rarity, and the police might find a little occupation in preventing it.

I am very pleased to hear that Police Sergeant Clarke of Earlestown, has been promoted to the position of Inspector of Weights and Measures in the Asht3n-under-Lyne division. Whilst express- ing my pleasure at the success of the popular sergeant,l must add my regrets at the, loss the town sustains by his removal. I feel certain in this I shall he joined by all the pressmen in the district, for in all his dealings with newspaper representa- tives he was ever courteous and genial, and always willing to afford the fullest information in regard to any cases coming within his knowledge. In this respect he will be missed.

THE ST. HELENS BURGLARIES.

LARGE RECOVERY OF STOLEN PROPERTY.

Hugh ileattie, a mechanic, of Claughton-street, St. Helens, was brought before Alderman Sinclair and Mr. Saarples at St. Helens on Thursday, and charged with having, between the 23rd and 25th March list, broken and entered the dwelling house of Mr. James Marsh, 5, Windle-street. and stolen therefrom jewellery and other articles, altogether to the value of £3O. Inspector Steele (in the absence of the Chief Constable) stated that between the dates named the inmates of 5, Windle-street, were away, and when they returned they found that the house had been broken open, and the prop( rty named taken. On Friday last Miss Battersby, housekeeper to Mr. Marsh, was passing a pawnshop in the town when she saw three brooches, which she recog- nised as her property, and which had been stolen. She gave informatian to the police, and it was found that they had been pledged by prisoner's wife. Mrs. Beattie was now in gaol under remand,and when prisoner found , that his wife was in custody he absconded. Detective-sergeant Strong stated that on Sunday morning last he went to prisoner's house, and found a great number of pawntickets and a lot of other property tlat- he could see had been stolen from various houses in the borough. He conveyed the property to the Town Hall, and it had been identified by various people as con- , nected with about fourteen robberies. On Wednesday lie apprehended Beattie at Sir Joseph Whitworth's en- gineeiing works, Gorton, near Manchester. At first he denied that his name was Beattie' and said he had not lived in St. Helens, but afterwards he admitted it. Inspector Steele asked for a remand until Tuesday next, when the husband and wife could be charged to gether. It was intimated that Mr. H. L. Riley had been in- Ftructed to appear on behalf of the Pawnbrokers' Associa- tion, and that he had no objection to the remand. Prisoner was accordingly remanded. The stolen property, now at the police office, is of a most tniscellaoeous description, and includes watches, brooches, and other jewellery, wearing apparel, writing materials, books,Bibles, an air gun, a pair of silent shoes arid a large quantity of keys. Numbers of people have visited and inspected the property, and have identified , artickis talon from their houses or offices.
